#6935. An Everyman’s Guide to Wearing a Necklace

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You already have a watch you like. This is the next step in your jewelry journey.

Men’s jewelry, I’ve learned, can be a slippery slope. I’ve been a watch guy since elementary school, if we’re generous enough to count a dual-sided compass/watch on a carabiner as a watch. In college I added a bracelet. I’ve been a ring guy for over ten years, thanks mostly to being married and partially to getting lunch drunk in Albuquerque, going ring shopping for my wife, and finding a sterling silver bear signet ring that I’ve worn nonstop since. I’ve had an earring for two years, thanks to a quick weekend in Miami and a well-timed Harrison Ford interview clip crossing my algorithm about getting his ear pierced in a Claire’s.

Recently, I’ve been curious about my personal final frontier of men’s jewelry—the necklace. The other listings above are all fairly low entry points, safe experiments in the world of men’s jewelry. The earring may be the exception, but this is 2026; one small silver hoop isn’t that daring, even in the Midwest. A necklace has never really been my thing. I haven’t felt the pull. That is, until this year.

For an unrelated project, I was breaking down some of the signature stylistic calling cards of Robert Redford. And there it was, across paparazzi pictures and movie stills and in memoriam roundups. In almost every photo, Redford has some kind of necklace, usually peeking from a precariously unbuttoned western shirt.

Further study of what we can safely call Hollywood’s golden age of off-duty style shows the same: McQueen and Newman and their contemporaries, swanning around on and off film, always wearing some sort of necklace or chain or pendant.

This is, of course, a common trap: to look at a famously handsome Hollywood star and think, yeah, all that’s separating me from THAT is a necklace. And sure, a good cheekbone and clavicle will get you pretty far in life. There’s no magic amulet here, nothing that will shazam you into leading-man looks. But there are a few lessons on styling from guys whose literal job it was to present themselves favorably to the world. And a few lessons on what necklaces to choose to help get there.

So, I put my professional online shopping style writing gig into overdrive, going deep into the Getty Images archives and confusing Google’s settings by repeatedly searching “Insert Hollywood Icon + shirtless” to glean some jewelry 101 tips from the professionals.

Instagram and (theoretically, since I don’t indulge) TikTok are full of maximalizing style trend chasers. Why wear one necklace when you can wear four? Why two rings when each finger could have two rings on it? Why dip your toes in the water when you can cannonball into the deep end? And sure, there’s some je ne sais quoi about just committing to something fully and completely out of the gate. But there’s also a lot of try-hard to it.

We’re channeling a more reserved, less #sponsoredpost era of dressing here that cares less about link clicks and more about how things look in the real world. So start simple and start with a chain. My favorite, and currently on daily rotation, is the Curb Chain from L.A.-based Bloedstone. Mine is sterling silver and in the inch length, which hits closer to the sternum than the neck. That’s the prescribed Hollywood A-lister way to wear it, but chart your own path if you want to. Miansai has a thicker 2mm Cuban chain that’s great too. And while we’re covering the M’s, Mejuri has one too. To my slight surprise, my home state watchmaker Shinola makes one, and less surprisingly so, Esquire-approved David Yurman has this giant one featuring a marketing campaign with Michael B. Jordan, who is picking up McQueen’s (and subsequently Brosnan’s) torch by adapting The Thomas Crown Affair.

There’s a whole vocabulary on link types and thickness and clasps out there. Don’t worry about that for now. Find something that calls to you and click add to cart. We’re starting slowly here.

Parting note. There are a lot of chains worn over T-shirts these days. To each their own, but the leading man’s move is to keep it under your shirt. If you want to show it off, just ditch the buttons.

A chain is simple, and sure, the greats wore them. But more often than not, they had something dangling from that chain. Medallion, pendant, a bottle opener in Cool Hand Luke. McQueen was often photographed, including in the pages of Esquire, wearing a St. Christopher medallion. Seeing as he’s the patron saint of travelers, St. Christopher medallions are having a bit of a cultural resurgence, partially because of their traditional association as a good-luck charm for travelers. There are some fun enamel ones on eBay, including these surfing-specific ones. Every once in a while, Nashville-based Americana experts Imogene + Willie will pull together a vintage edit of St. Christopher medallions that always sell out in around 24 hours. Speaking of Nashville, if you’re going to go new, go with Yearly Co. The gold jewelry experts have a 14k version of the iconography with a pitch-perfect 24-inch chain. I can attest to the quality—I gave my wife the brand’s diamond huggies and she wears them almost every day. As the grandson of a Protestant minister, I steer away from the saints out of genetic loyalty to Luther’s 95 Theses, but I recently picked up this Perseverance medallion from the brand, inspired by a vintage signet ring. It scratches the McQueen itch with flying colors.

Secondhand gold is also an option. Alloy Market recently came onto my radar, and I’m browsing it daily for myself and gifts for my wife. They’ve got a small but mighty collection of certified men’s items with pendants and medallions you can mix and match. All of the quality without any of the markup.

Don’t limit yourself to just a medallion, though. Get a little funky. Redford did for a while with this turquoise number that somehow looked even cooler hitting under the sternum. Shinola’s got an option there too. Bali-based Salty Dagger has an Odysseus-approved Greek mythology piece. And why not try a locket, like this one from Pattaraphan. Miansai has a great pendant. Get fancy with the Prevaut diamond ring deal. When in doubt, steal from the girls. I got this With Clarity birthstone necklace for my wife that I might take a swing at myself.

Peyote Bird High Desert Sterling Silver Necklace

Shinola 21mm Turquoise Inlay Tag Necklace

From the abbreviated/slightly bastardized Shakespeare: “All the world’s a stage and one man in his time plays many parts.” Jewelry can feel like a risk, even if you’re following some sort of contrived guide to styling and buying it. Sometimes we pretend until we’re comfortable actually being. Redford and McQueen and company were actors. Pretending was their legacy, and they were good at it. Take a leaf from their book and get dressed for the curtain call.

#6934. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

#6930. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6924. The Vintage Features That EVERY Homebuyer Wants Right Now, According to Experts

These are the authentic details that make a home extra special.

Pictured above: Designer Annie Kern showcased breathtaking stained glass windows throughout this historic home in Kansas City, Missouri.

I grew up in a family captivated—if not downright obsessed—by homes with character. My parents always took the long way home, winding slowly through our town's historic neighborhoods to admire the details on every house: immaculate stone walls, whimsical wrought-iron gates, ivy-covered façades, and shingles weathered to silvery perfection. Those drives became a kind of education, and each architectural flourish inspired the home I grew up in.

In recent decades, though, the real estate market has been flooded with sleek new builds and smart-home conveniences, which in turn have dimmed the prominence of intricate vintage details. Even worse, the structures often look incongruous with their surroundings. Prefab townhomes now rise from former parking lots, farmhouse-style mansions perch improbably on California sand, and shiny concrete slabs have replaced weathered shingles deep in the New England woods.

But, as with all trends, we’ve begun to circle back. Many experts are now seeing that buyers are rediscovering the charm of craftsmanship and are looking to prioritize authentic vintage features that just can't be faked. Ahead, we spoke with several pros about the special home details their clients are falling for—and what it takes to score your own home ripe with vintage appeal in a competitive market.

A renewed appreciation for period details—think original windows, layered molding, and aged floors—is something Peter Michael, founder of Piere Michel Brokerage, is witnessing firsthand.

“People are drawn to homes that feel lived in and loved,” he says. “Original craftsmanship is what this new wave of buyers is really looking for. It starts with the arches in the hallways to the exposed brick fireplaces—the list can go on and on.”

After years of streamlined interiors and minimalist façades, buyers are once again searching for texture, depth, and evidence of the human hand. “Original hardwood floors, high ceilings, stained glass, exposed brick, and vintage light fixtures are usually the things that pop out,” Michael adds. “They make a space feel rooted, not manufactured.”

That nostalgia isn’t confined to historic properties. Jackie Mosher, cofounder and COO of Dzinly, says even new builds are embracing time-worn charm through exterior details that nod to the past.

“I’m seeing a growing number of new homes incorporating decorative eaves and exposed rafters,” she says. “These details, once hallmarks of early Craftsman homes, are being reinterpreted today on Modern and Transitional houses. They add depth and visible craftsmanship to otherwise simple designs—bridging the gap between timeless tradition and contemporary simplicity.”

Mosher notes that stucco, once reserved for homes in warmer climates, is reemerging in cooler regions. “It’s fascinating to see homeowners in the Midwest and Northeast choosing stucco—it requires more effort to install because of the temperature fluctuations, but people are willing to make that investment for the aesthetic,” she explains. And while the material palette may draw from the past, the execution is crisp: thinner window frames, smooth finishes, and clean horizontal lines inspired by midcentury design.

The goal, Mosher says, isn’t replication—it’s reinterpretation. “People aren’t trying to recreate the past, but to blend nostalgic details with modern forms,” she explains. “That’s what makes a home feel truly timeless.”

Of course, today’s buyers want the best of both worlds. “They want the vintage look, but they can’t live without modern technology,” Michael says. “A smart thermostat is almost a must, along with stainless steel appliances. It’s a blend of things.”

That blend extends to floor plans as well. While classic Tudors, Craftsman bungalows, and brownstones are perpetually in demand, interior layouts are evolving to suit modern lifestyles. “Popcorn ceilings and small, isolated kitchens are becoming a no-no,” Michael says. “Buyers love a good open layout and want a clean-cut look with a mixture of craftsmanship.”

It’s that mixture—the contrast of the hand-hewn with the high-tech—that defines the moment.

Mosher believes the secret to timeless appeal lies in restraint. “For a home to feel enduring, the design shouldn’t lean too heavily on trends or overly bold choices,” she says. “Muted, nature-based tones—warm whites, taupes, and sages—create that sense of heritage. And contrasting the trim with the cladding helps define the home, grounding it in a way that feels classic.”

After decades of neutral sameness, it seems buyers are once again craving personality. They want arches instead of corners, warmth instead of gloss, and craftsmanship that invites you to look twice.

#6920. 13 Things You Should Toss ASAP If You’ve Lived in Your Home for More Than 5 Years

You'll feel a wave of relief once you do it.

It may seem like you just moved into your new home or apartment yesterday. But in a flash, five years have passed and you’re out of storage and feeling overwhelmed. Most of us know it doesn’t take long for our belongings to pile up.

“By year five, the ‘endless storage’ honeymoon is over,” says Ann Lightfoot, cofounder, DDH Home Organizing & Move Management. “Your stuff has multiplied, your square footage hasn’t, and you now know how you actually live in your space.”

But now is the ideal time for a thoughtful edit. “Taking stock after five years gives us a chance to see what's actually taking up space, what we're still using, and what we've simply gotten used to storing,” says Laura Kinsella, founder of Urban OrgaNYze. “Our careers, families, routines, and interests can change dramatically, but the things we've accumulated don't automatically change with us.”

Decluttering also enhances good design. “To me, a beautifully designed home isn't about how much you can fit into it. It's about being intentional with what you choose to keep,” says Kerrie Kelly, NKBA, FASID, CEO and creative director of Kerrie Kelly Studio, and NKBA 2026 National Board Chair. “Editing isn't about deprivation; it's about making room for what you actually love and use.”

If you don’t know where to begin, start small. “Edit by category rather than trying to tackle the entire house in a weekend,” says Kelly. “Start with one drawer, one cabinet, or one closet. Take everything out, clean the space, and put back only what deserves to be there. It's amazing how quickly those small decisions transform the way a home feels. Empty space is a luxury, too!”

Ahead, here’s what design and organizational experts say you should get rid of if you’ve been in your home five years or more.

“Five years of conferences, events, fundraisers, and corporate gifting can leave you with an impressive collection of tote bags, reusable water bottles, and branded merchandise you never actually set out to own,” says Kinsella. Keep what you genuinely like and use, and pass along the extras.

Most manuals live online now, and that tangle of chargers likely powers nothing in your house. Recycle them responsibly, says Lightfoot.

“Threadbare towels, mismatched sheets, and extra bedding you never reach for take up valuable storage space,” says Kelly. “Keep a thoughtfully curated collection of beautiful, functional linens and recycle the worn-out ones where possible.”

“If your spin shoes haven't been touched since lockdown and the exercise bike has become ‘the chair’ in your bedroom, you may be dedicating valuable space to a workout routine that's no longer part of your life,” says Kinsella. Sell or donate equipment you no longer use so you have room for the activities you truly enjoy now.

Craft kits, furniture you planned to refinish, the bread maker and so on: “If someday hasn’t arrived in five years, give yourself permission to let it go, guilt-free,” says Lightfoot.

“Those little objects that have been sitting on a shelf for years can become visual clutter,” says Kelly. “I love a meaningful collection, but every piece should earn its place. Sometimes removing three things makes the one beautiful object remaining look even better.”

“That rug or wall art you swore would work ‘somewhere’ has spent five years leaning against a wall. It’s time to release it to a home where it fits,” says Lightfoot.

Hang on to a small labeled paint sample for touch-ups—and maybe one tile. But the rest, including the dried-up products or the five half-empty cans in the garage or basement, can go to your town’s hazardous waste drop-off, says Lightfoot.

This is a no-judgment zone: “If the avocado slicer, specialty pan, or oversized appliance hasn't made an appearance in years, it probably doesn't deserve prime real estate in your kitchen,” says Kelly. Ditto for duplicate kitchen tools.

“Whether it's a juicer, red light mask, or massage tool, we've all had that new wellness routine we're completely committed to for a few weeks before life gets busy and the habit quietly falls away,” says Kinsella. “The problem is that the gadget doesn't disappear when the enthusiasm does, and five years later, you may have an entire cabinet devoted to routines that lasted a month.” It’s fine to try something new to learn it wasn't for you, but you don't have to keep storing the equipment forever.

Maybe it's an extra accent chair that collects clothes or a side table that's always in the way. Just because something fits physically doesn't mean it belongs. Give your rooms space to breathe and your furniture room to shine, says Kelly.

Purge the old sunscreen, crusty nail polish, expired makeup, shampoo you don’t like, and half-used cleaners that have piled up in your bathroom. Toss them safely and enjoy the instant breathing room, says Lightfoot.

“If you've lived in your home for five years and still have boxes you never unpacked, it's a pretty good indication that you haven't needed most of what's inside on a regular basis,” says Kinsella. “Open the box, decide what's worth keeping, and find those belongings a proper home so you're not continuing to live around a move that’s already happened.”

#6917. 7 Desert Boots That Can Survive the Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Despite the name, these chukkas are built to be worn in all conditions.

Winter weather can get ugly. If it’s not wind and rain, it’s slushy sidewalks and unexpected snow flurries. You obviously need shoes that can handle a little precipitation, but the key phrase here is a little. You don’t want to stomp around in heavy winter boots, especially if we want to look nice. Dress shoes are a tough fit in winter, as are dress boots. Funny enough, between December and March, you know what our go-to footwear becomes? Desert boots. Technically chukka boots, but a few desert boots make the cut here.

But that’s something to note. The Esquire office does not use the term interchangeably—but the Internet does, so we’re going along with it. We know some of you—nerds, mostly—have stopped reading this article because you don’t think we know the difference between the two. We’re well aware of the square and rectangle-ism: All desert boots are chukkas, not all chukkas are desert boots.

If you’re not a nerd and want to learn something new, chukkas are ankle-high boots with open lacing and two or three pairs of eyelets, often made with leather uppers—leather or rubber soled. Desert boots are a subsection of this silhouette that typically features a suede upper (nubuck, sometimes) with lighter-colored, more flexible crepe soles. They are not as winterized—suede, especially, is known to take a beating from moisture—hence the name. The details vary here and there, but both styles can work for winter if you choose wisely.

We found seven pairs of chukka boots—yes, some of which are desert boots—with weather-friendly features, from water-resistant uppers to sturdy rubber soles. Most have both, so you can find a pair that looks sharp without leaving you entirely at the mercy of the winter weather forecast.

These chukka boots might as well be dress boots. They look sharp no matter what outfit you pair them with, and the ultra-refined, premium calfskin leather upper is naturally water-resistant; to a point, that is—we wouldn’t wear them in the pouring rain.

They’re very comfortable thanks to a soft, leather lining on the inside and open-cell foam insoles that provide supportive cushioning. And the flexible outsoles made from XL Extralight injection molding are a nice touch that give the boot some lightweight flexibility.

For a classic pair of chukka boots that come at a good price and have a wide range of colors and sizes, Thursday Boot Co. is the way to go. These chukkas come in a variety of colors and materials, but our favorite has to be the Mocha colorway in weather-safe suede. It’s rare to find a pair of suede shoes you can actually get wet without ruining, but these ones can brave the weather while still looking great. And with studded rubber outsoles, you have some extra traction and durability. If you want even more traction and durability in the soles, try one of the Rugged & Resilient styles with anti-slip wedge rubber outsoles.

Here’s another suede option you can actually wear during the winter; Sabah treats the suede upper of this boot to be water resistant, but you can still add a suede treatment for extra protection if you want to. These boots are handmade in Monte San Giusto, Italy, and you can trust that they were made with lots of skill and care. The insole and midsole are made from vegetable tanned leather, so they end up forming to your feet once you wear them, making them extra comfortable.

If you like the look of a gum-sole sneaker, you might like how it looks on a boot, too. With these desert boots, SeaVees blends the classic gum-sole look with the more rugged desert boot style, and the result is a versatile boot that can withstand pretty much any weather. With water-resistant rugged oiled leather uppers and natural rubber soles, these guys can get you through quite a lot.

These are going to be the least weather-ready on this list. You don’t want to take them out in a snowstorm, but damn do they look good. Imagine these worn to a holiday party, worn with a nice pair of chinos and fair-isle sweater.

The suede upper is a true not-water-resistant suede. But they do have durable rubber soles. The stitched moc-toe is a fun detail that makes these chukka boots stand out from the rest of the pack. Drake’s notes that these boots run small, so they recommend sizing up by a half-size when ordering online.

We’re starting to get pretty far away from the chukka heart, but you can see the chukka-shaped vamp and the ankle-y shape. Plus, when we think of rugged workwear boots, we think of Timberland. The upper is made from waterproof leather and has a TimberDry waterproof membrane for extra protection, and you can see that Gripstick rubber outsole gives you some distance from the ground and provides great traction as well.

And finally, we can’t talk about chukka boots without mentioning Clarks. The original Clarks desert boot was designed in 1949 and inspired by the rough suede and crepe-soled boots worn by off-duty British soldiers during WWII (as well as the shoes worn by polo players in India and South African veldskoen shoes). Today, the brand’s original style is still going strong, and you can get a pair for under $100.

#6915. Why Do Some Famous Men Keep Gaining Fans After Serious Allegations?

Russell Brand’s sentencing raises a bleak question about accountability.

On November 9, Russell Brand was sentenced to over 10 years in prison after being found guilty on one count of rape and three counts of sexual assault. The four-week trial involved six women, who came forward after four others made public allegations against Brand via a 2023 investigation by The Times and The Sunday Times and supporting Dispatches documentary. Some accounts described his behavior as an “open secret” in the entertainment industry, spanning decades.

Responding to the verdict, women’s domestic abuse charity Refuge said, “The guilty verdicts in this trial represent long overdue justice for the survivors whose lives have been devastated—sending an important signal that survivors’ voices matter, and abusers will be held accountable. At the same time, we know that in this trial not all survivors received a guilty verdict.”

The organization added that the lengthy justice process, retraumatizing trial experience, and mixed verdicts reflected “the unacceptable barriers faced by survivors across the board,” adding that conviction rates for violence against women and girls offenses remain “alarmingly low” as a whole.

With that in mind, is it any wonder that Brand has a combined following of almost 24 million across various social media platforms, even reportedly gaining close to 2 million followers since the accusations became public three years ago? Supporters stood outside court for him daily, holding signs pledging their devotion. And he’s far from the only famous man accused of major wrongdoing who managed to retain a huge audience or return to work (with some even going on to have bigger successes post-allegations).

It raises an ugly question: Why can some men keep growing their platforms, careers, and bank balances after serious allegations emerge? And who’s responsible—the fans who stick around, the platforms that boost them, or the normalized misogyny that makes it so accepted to look the other way?

Brand’s A-list life may be over—he told the court that former associates would no longer “touch him with a barge pole”—but he’s still managed to pivot, as so many accused men seem to, down the “reformed by religion” route (see also: Sean “Diddy” Combs clutching a Bible on his way into court after a video of him beating his ex, Cassie Ventura, in a hotel hallway spread like wildfire. He later posted an apology video and was convicted on two prostitution-related charges).

In 2023, Parliament’s Culture, Media and Sport Committee raised concerns about whether Brand could profit from content about the allegations. YouTube said it suspended his channel’s monetization that September, but the content remains live, allowing Brand to spread harmful messaging (often referencing God’s will and how he’s been wrongly persecuted by “empire media,” all while promoting crypto betting sites).

One popular comment, under a video posted mid-trial, at least hits the nail on the head: “On trial for rape but he sits there baring his chest in a leopard print nightgown quoting Bible verses at the camera while playing with a cat. Bizarre doesn’t cover it.”

You don’t have to look far for other examples. Chris Brown pleaded guilty to assaulting Rihanna in 2009, and it would seem his music career carried on without much impact (even after another ex-partner, model Karrueche Tran, filed for a restraining order against him in 2017, testifying under oath that Brown had threatened to kill her—years after he’d completed a court-mandated domestic violence counseling program).

Most recently, his Breezy Bowl XX tour sold 1.93 million tickets across 48 shows in Europe and North America last year, taking more than $285 million. Last month, Brown was given a suspended eight-month prison sentence after pleading guilty to attacking a man in a London nightclub. Fans cheered as he left court.

UFC fighter Conor McGregor was found liable by a jury for assaulting Nikita Hand after a civil trial at Ireland’s High Court. Hand’s lawyers had accused him of “brutally raping and battering” her, and she heartbreakingly recalled how she had to have a tampon removed by a doctor afterward, as the assault was so brutal. The director of public prosecutions had previously decided not to pursue a criminal case, and McGregor denied the allegations and returned to sports.

None of these cases are identical, and of course, being accused is not the same as being found guilty. But with all these men, there were legal findings of some form of wrongdoing—and plenty within their fan bases have only seemed to lock in harder thereafter.

Women speak out. The story breaks. Then the man’s audience keeps growing. And it all begs the bleak question: If a conviction doesn’t even stop the numbers rising, what will? Maybe, just maybe, we can dare to hope that Brand’s sentencing is finally the start of this desperately needed change.

#6913. I Went to the World's Biggest Furniture Trade Show—These 6 Trends Are About to Be Everywhere

The furniture profiles, patterns, and palettes you’re about to see everywhere.

Twice a year, High Point, North Carolina, becomes the home industry’s giant showroom, where furniture and design brands unveil new collections for retailers, interior designers, and buyers to see and source. At House Beautiful, we’re your boots on the ground, looking for the ideas that may travel from showroom to store—and into your home next.

This fall, Market made a case for decorating with more character and muddier hues: green moved from the forest floor to the showroom floor, florals claimed entire sofas, and upholstery played with everything from a flash of leg to a full fabric wrap. Add wood-backed furniture as an accent, garden-inspired lighting, and a generous swish of fringe, and the message was clear: The details are ready to take center stage.

This year, green was the unofficial color of Market. Benjamin Moore and Behr had already put the hue on the radar with their 2027 Colors of the Year: Webster Green, a confident mid-tone balancing blue’s coolness with yellow’s warmth, and Grounded, a mossy olive softened by earthy brown undertones. Then came the showroom echoes. Versions of green, from earthy vegetable shades to deeper, moodier tones, appeared nearly everywhere. Kathy Kuo even created a “Music Room” (shown in our opening image) for Rowe Furniture inspired by the verdant shade. The appeal is easy to understand: Green brings the natural world indoors, lending a sense of calm and grounding a space without draining it of personality.

The design industry’s obsession with prints is now a love letter to botanicals. Florals erupted across Market in a myriad of scales, from small, almost sun-bleached botanicals to overscaled, tapestry-adjacent blooms. Even the tiniest prints made a surprisingly bold statement when carried across an entire sofa; the larger blooms brought the feel of a faded heirloom into full view. Both have that grandmotherly, another-era quality, but the impact is anything but timid. Yes, it’s a major design commitment, but why play it safe with a solid, barely-there pattern? It may not be for the faint of design, but we are all in a sofa fully dressed in flowers.

Here’s a furniture detail that changes the whole silhouette: Brands are letting wood take over the outside back—or sides—of upholstered seating. Baker’s sofa makes the move hard to miss, with a broad wood shell framing the piece. It’s a little like “inside-out” upholstery, where the outside gets its own treatment, but here the wood gives the piece architectural shape and presence. As floor plans open up and sofas float in family and living rooms, the back is no longer hidden against a wall. It’s part of the view, and part of how the seating area takes shape, making it all the more important that it feels worth looking at.

Upholstery continues to take its cues from fashion, with a focus on mixing silhouettes: Mini skirts that let those gams show; full, ballooning skirts with all the best parts of an ’80s prom dress; and what we lovingly call body-con upholstery, where the entire chair is wrapped head to toe in one fabric.

The appeal goes beyond a good fashion metaphor, though. Each treatment changes the chair’s proportions: a flash of leg keeps it light, a generous skirt adds volume, and an all-over wrap turns both seat and base into one continuous form, with no leg finish to choose or worry about. Each has its own distinct style POV, making your shopping choices that much more complicated, but in the best way.

If garden motifs aren’t your thing, don’t look up. The floral revival isn’t limited to upholstery and wallpaper; it’s reaching the ceiling, too. Visual Comfort & Co.’s Fairoaks collection, designed by Fisher Weisman, includes a chandelier, sconce, and mini flush mount. Sculptural branches and twigs cleverly disguise the chandelier’s ceiling cord, preserving the romance of the design. At Hudson Valley Lighting, a tulip-inspired chandelier brings a more literal bloom overhead. Both offer a way to bring the garden indoors through shape and silhouette, without committing an entire room to pattern.

Sometimes, all it takes is a little bullion fringe. The thick, tightly twisted cords fall in a dense, weighty row, adding plush texture and a satisfying swish to a skirted chair or sofa. It nods to the Victorian parlor, but stops short of period-drama excess; against a cleaner silhouette or in a spare room, the old-world trim feels playful and unexpectedly fresh. Consider it the design equivalent of a great pair of earrings: a little extra, in exactly the right place.

#6908. Is Your Skincare Routine Breaking You Out? Here’s How to Tell If You’ve Damaged Your Skin Barrier

And acne experts share how to repair compromised skin.

If you’ve stepped foot in a Sephora recently, odds are you’ve witnessed tweens and teens flooding the skincare aisles and filling up baskets to the brim. “I saw this one on TikTok,” a seemingly no-older-than-12-year-old girl says to a friend as they purchase bottles of serums designed to smooth their mother’s and grandmother’s lines. The beauty marketplace has transformed into a social haven, and teens have moved away from buying lip gloss and face wash and are now purchasing potent, anti-aging formulas that could be doing far more harm than good.

And it’s not just the under-18 crowd. Between TikTok Shop, skinfluencers, and the endless stream of celebrity beauty launches, we’re all being encouraged to add another cream, exfoliant, or treatment to our routines. And for those of us dealing with acne, that temptation can be especially strong—when you’re trying to clear persistent breakouts, it’s easy to convince yourself that every buzzy ingredient might finally be the one that finally works. But piling on too many active ingredients can backfire, irritating your skin and compromising the very barrier that helps keep your complexion balanced and healthy.

“Consumers are more educated and enthusiastic about skincare than ever before, which is awesome, but there’s also this constant pursuit of the latest and greatest ingredient or product,” says Stephanie Criscione, licensed aesthetician and senior director of education and clinic at Face Reality. “Somewhere along the way, we’ve started equating more products and stronger ingredients with better results.”

Your skin barrier is responsible for retaining moisture and helping protect your complexion from external irritants. When it’s compromised, the skin can become dry, tight, red, and unusually sensitive. For people with acne-prone skin, that irritation can exacerbate breakouts, especially when the products meant to treat blemishes are also contributing to irritation.

So how do you know when your skincare routine has crossed the line from helping your acne to potentially making it worse? And if you’ve already gone a little too far with the actives, how do you repair your skin barrier while still managing breakouts? I spoke with skincare professionals about the signs of a damaged skin barrier, the acne treatments to be mindful of, and how to build a routine that supports both clearer skin and a healthy complexion.

Yes, one of the biggest misconceptions in skincare is that more steps are better. In reality, even well-researched ingredients have limits, and using too many at once can overwhelm your skin. This is especially important when you’re treating acne, since many of the ingredients commonly used to address breakouts can also cause irritation when they’re overused or combined incorrectly.

“Typically, this goes for certain AHAs like glycolic acid, retinoids, and benzoyl peroxide, which can all be phenomenal ingredients used to effectively treat acne,” says Samantha Susca, NYC-based licensed aesthetician and founder of Refinery4. “But there is a fine line between proper usage and misuse.”

And it’s not just about how many products you’re using. The way skincare is marketed can also make it difficult to separate what your skin actually needs from what brands want you to buy. “There are certain brands that I am noticing with a ton of great marketing, but that are actually being over-marketed to certain clients who are not being taught how to use certain ingredients properly, and they’re causing more damage,” she says.

Celebrity-founded brands can add another layer to that confusion, especially when consumers assume a product alone can recreate a celebrity's complexion. Susca says there’s a lot more that goes into maintaining that skin than what’s inside the A-lister-promoted bottle.

“Using a product that a celebrity is selling is not going to give you their skin,” she explains. “What you’re not seeing or getting in that bottle is the tens—if not hundreds—of thousands of dollars of on-call dermatologists’ and aestheticians’ work, prescriptions, lasers, peels, and more.”

This is particularly relevant for younger consumers, who may be reaching for products designed to address concerns they haven’t yet developed. Susca says she’s seeing strong ingredients like retinoids used frequently on teen and preteen skin. While acne treatments can be appropriate for younger patients who need them, a complicated routine filled with multiple active ingredients isn’t necessarily the best way to get breakouts under control. The right treatment depends on the individual’s skin and should be introduced thoughtfully, with professional guidance when needed.

Your skin will usually let you know when something isn’t working. “It may not show an effect on the skin barrier immediately, but after a few days, your face may start to feel tight, itchy, uncomfortable, and sometimes red,” Susca explains. “These are signs of an impaired skin barrier, which is something that can take weeks to months of repair and reregulation.”

Breakouts can also become harder to manage when your skin is irritated. A compromised barrier doesn’t automatically cause acne, but the inflammation and sensitivity that come with barrier disruption can exacerbate existing blemishes. “Even someone with acne or oily skin can become dehydrated when the barrier isn’t functioning properly,” Criscione explains. You might also notice that products you previously tolerated, including your usual acne treatments, suddenly sting or leave your skin feeling flaky or uncomfortable.

If your skin is feeling irritated, it is not the time to introduce more steps. Susca says to “remove all actives and keep the same simple routine morning and night,” so your skin can begin to readjust.

For anyone managing acne, yes, this can feel counterintuitive. Taking a break from exfoliants or acne treatments might make you nervous about triggering more breakouts, but continuing to apply products that are irritating your skin will make the situation harder to manage.

Karan Lal, MD, double-board-certified adult, pediatric, and cosmetic dermatologist, suggests paring things back to a few essentials. “I recommend a simplified routine built around a few evidence-based ingredients,” he says. “A gentle cleanser helps remove excess oil without stripping the skin, a noncomedogenic moisturizer containing ceramides, glycerin, or other humectants will support barrier function, and sunscreen is essential, because many acne treatments increase sensitivity and post-inflammatory pigmentation can be exacerbated by UV exposure.”

How long it takes your skin to recover depends on how irritated it is. Susca notes that repairing a compromised skin barrier can take weeks to months, so don’t expect your complexion to bounce back overnight. If you’re experiencing persistent irritation, painful inflammation, or breakouts that aren’t improving, consult a dermatologist. They can help determine whether you’re dealing with acne, irritation from your products, or both, and recommend a treatment plan that addresses the underlying concerns.

Once your skin has settled after a few weeks or months, you can consider reintroducing treatments one at a time. For people using retinoids to manage acne, Dr. Lal recommends starting slowly. “Topical retinoids should be introduced gradually; you can begin with two or three nights per week and increase frequency as tolerated.”

He also recommends being strategic with other acne treatments, rather than layering multiple formulas just because each one targets a different concern. “Benzoyl peroxide can be used strategically, and lower concentrations or short-contact formulations may be useful for sensitive skin,” he explains. “Azelaic acid is another excellent option because it has anti-inflammatory, comedolytic, and pigment-reducing properties.”

There’s no milestone birthday when you suddenly need a more involved routine. Your skin type, concerns, and tolerance should determine which ingredients you use. And if you’re dealing with acne, that doesn’t necessarily mean you need to keep adding stronger treatments as you get older. The goal is to find an approach that addresses breakouts while keeping your skin barrier in good shape, but if you want a simple guideline, keep reading for what our experts recommend.

Teenagers should be the age group keeping it the simplest. “Generally, teens need only to cleanse, moisturize, and apply SPF in the day time,” Susca explains. “Sometimes a weekly or biweekly exfoliant may be appropriate,” specifically if they’re dealing with acne.

Criscione agrees. “In your teens, the focus is mostly on establishing healthy habits, gentle cleansing, daily sunscreen, and managing acne if it’s an issue.”

If teens are dealing with persistent breakouts, a dermatologist can help determine which acne treatments are appropriate for their specific skin’s needs. You definitely don’t need to introduce a full lineup of exfoliating acids and serums.

Criscione says this decade “is really about prevention, protecting against UV damage, and introducing targeted ingredients when needed to address specific concerns.” This is where you can start to get into exfoliating formulas and protective serums.

Susca suggests building on a basic routine with an AHA, such as glycolic or lactic acid, or a BHA, like salicylic acid, along with an antioxidant serum such as vitamin C.

Into your 30s, Susca says “a retinol and eye cream can be added in as well,” and recommends preventative treatments like microdermabrasion and microcurrent.

Criscione notes that hormonal changes can bring new skin concerns during this time. “By your 30s, you may start noticing differences in skin texture, pigmentation, or fine lines, so ingredients like retinoids and antioxidants can be helpful,” she says.

Acne can also continue into adulthood or appear for the first time during this decade. If you’re experiencing recurring breakouts, especially if your skin is also becoming drier or more sensitive, it’s worth revisiting your routine rather than automatically reaching for stronger exfoliants. A dermatologist can help you find acne treatments that address breakouts while accounting for your skin’s changing needs.

As your skin changes with age, hydration and barrier support may become bigger priorities. Criscione says, “In your 40s, 50s, and beyond, hormonal changes can influence hydration, barrier function, and skin elasticity, making moisture retention and barrier support increasingly important.”

And if you’re still managing acne, getting older doesn’t mean you have to give up effective treatments. “As skin becomes drier and more sensitive with age, the goal is not to eliminate effective acne treatment, it is to deliver that treatment in a way the skin can tolerate,” Dr. Lal says. “For patients in their 40s and beyond, the skin may be drier or more sensitive, so I may recommend a lower-strength retinoid, less frequent application, or a more supportive moisturizing regimen.” This may include richer night creams or barrier creams for added hydration.

If you’re dealing with acne, it’s tempting to keep adding products in the hope that one more active ingredient will finally clear your skin. But overusing exfoliants, retinoids, and acne treatments can compromise your skin barrier, leaving your complexion irritated and making existing breakouts harder to manage. Plus, it’s always important to check for noncomedogenic ingredients in your products if you’re acne-prone.

Pay attention to how your skin responds to formulas, especially if new blemishes appear alongside tightness, redness, or sensitivity. The goal is to find a regimen that treats your acne while supporting your skin barrier, because clearer skin shouldn’t have to come at the expense of a healthy complexion.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things skincare and has dealt with acne for the last five years. For this story, she consulted acne experts for all their insight on skin barrier damage and how your skincare routine can impact acne.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6907. These 9 Brands Are Making the Best Dark-Wash Jeans on the Market

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Moody, rich, and sophisticated—everything you want to project with your dressing.

In the world of denim, think of dark wash jeans like a good Scotch. It exists within a broader umbrella, but it’s a little more weathered and more mature. In the grand scheme of jeans, you really can’t go wrong when it comes to different washes. For clarity, we use dark wash to describe lightly washed dark jeans, raw sanforized jeans, and the real nerdy loomstate shrink-to-fit stuff. Why? Because they are all, visually, dark wash. For us, it’s exactly how you should buy your jeans.

Jeans inherently skew casual. But the subdued color of dark wash allows it to pass for something a little more refined. Where other denim gets processed and faded after being dyed, dark wash hasn’t—letting it keep that subdued inky hue that veers slightly toward “polished trouser” territory. They’re more rugged than a pair of slacks, but just formal and sophisticated enough that you can probably wear them around the office as a sort of nonchalantly edgy work pant.

You won’t need to hunt far or hard for quality and stylish dark washes. Most, if not all, of the Esquire-editor-approved jeans and denim brands also offer darker colors—making it as easy as buying your favorite sweater in another color. Find your favorite below.

Rag & Bone knows what it’s good at and sticks to it. (It’s denim...they’re good at denim.) Our reviewers especially like how the brand approaches its sizing and fits. It’s a super simple and straightforward number system that starts at 1 and goes up to 4. Fit 1 offers a slimmer silhouette, while Fit 4 gives that classic straight look.

For their dark wash—which Rag & Bone calls “Solomon”—the brand uses its very own rb Selvedge, which it developed in partnership with one of Japan's premier denim mills. This will help the jeans naturally develop a nice patina over time to really show just how much you love your denim.

If you ask Esquire shopping editor Luke Guillory what jeans to buy, he’s going to tell you these. He’s already waxed poetic about Wrangler’s original Cowboy Cut in our denim buying guide, so we’ll just cut to the chase: they’re super flattering and at an ultra-competitive price (though he advises bigger guys should opt for the more relaxed cut).

If you’re looking for something under $100, Gap’s more than likely where it’s at. The retailer has spent a ton of time and effort in reupping its basics—jeans especially. The brand’s classic straight silhouette is, at this point in time, better than the offering from the big-name brand that starts with “L” and rhymes with WiFi.

People hunt for vintage Levi’s for a reason. These 505s are a reproduction of the brand’s iconic style for 1967. As Luke describes in our denim buying guide, the fit will “hug up top, drape down low.” “They're universally flattering and classic cut, so you don't have to worry about them going out of style,” he says. “As long as they're holding on, you'll look good.”

We’ve already rounded up a few Todd Snyder pieces we’d recommend buying, and these dark indigo worn wash jeans are one more thing to add to the list. Available in three different fits—slim, straight, and relaxed—it’s made from an all-cotton denim that balances comfort and durability.

This denim from Ralph Lauren’s Americana sublabel is an absolute classic. It’s got a distinctly Western feel with its deep 13.75 oz selvedge denim, while still offering that sort of rugged sophistication you want from a dark wash jean.

Little known fact, but Acne Studios actually started out with jeans. Over 20 years later, it’s still their bread and butter—with funky pieces that embody a “rock and roll edge,” as Luke describes them. This groovy wide-legged pair especially gives off a sort of “Lenny-Kravitz-meets-Steven-Tyler” air of vintage swagger.

We’re long-time fans of Buck Mason. As Esquire contributor Matthew Medendrop describes, the brand has been “quietly doing some of the most exciting things in contemporary menswear” for the last few years—and their denim is no exception. The brand’s “full saddle” jeans take inspiration from the classic vintage blue jeans, think Levi’s 501s circa like the ‘60s. Pair that with the deep dark wash, and you got one sexy pair of jeans.

3Sixteen is one of the premier brands for Esquire’s denim nerds. It gets into the nitty-gritty stuff we care about, but since it’s based here in North America—and not in Japan—it’s a lot easier to get your hands on. To break down the naming, RS means relaxed straight, what we think is the easiest to wear from the brand. The 100xk tag is the type of denim—custom woven for the brand by Kabuki Mills. It’s rough at first, be warned, but it softens and fades like no other.

#6904. This Unexpected Antique Is Making a Major Comeback—and It Might Be Hiding in Your Grandma’s Art Collection

This centuries-old style brings fresh character to the walls.

Vintage art adds soul to a space. There’s just something about that story and patina that lends itself to more personality for your walls. While oil paintings and tiny art have certainly had their time to shine, there’s another antique art form that’s taking center stage: They’re called reverse glass paintings.

Popular in India in the 18th and 19th centuries, reverse glass paintings are illustrations that have been hand-painted onto the back of glass, giving them a painterly, colorful look. Annalise Holasz, founder of vintage art gallery Hue + Holasz, says that these works have a “really naïve” quality since each is painted by hand, and the “little variations make each one special.”

Holasz also notes that their frames, often made from reclaimed wood, are particularly special, since they provide a charming, aged, and even battered appearance. These artworks can showcase visuals like flowers, plants, Indian princes, boats, and shells, as Holasz says, “but animals are probably the most common,” and include birds, fish, sea creatures, and jungle animals.

To learn more about reverse glass paintings, we tapped the experts, who are sharing what to look for when starting your own collection.

Alexander Widener, owner of Widener Company, believes that reverse glass paintings are rising in popularity because “they capture the spirit of craft and yesteryear that so many of us are craving in our increasingly digital world.” It also doesn’t hurt that earlier this year, designer Tory Burch gave Vogue a tour of her Paris apartment, where reverse glass paintings were spotlighted in her powder room.

“I think people are drawn to how fun and playful they are,” Holasz says. “There’s often a subject that feels personal, and the combination of vibrant color and a characterful wooden frame appeals.”

Melanie Lissack of Melanie Lissack Interiors believes that reverse glass paintings are trending because they are eclectic, unique, and vintage in style while still having a modern twist.

While current-day pieces can look remarkably similar to the bygone versions, Lissack thinks they just don’t carry the same charm as the antique pieces—as striking as they are. Those in the know don’t look at the artwork first; they look at the frame. For instance, Lissack is on the lookout for slightly worn frames.

Widener points out that if you’re questing after particularly old reverse glass paintings, you should look for bubbles in the glass, a sign that the glass was handblown. This was a standard practice up until the early 20th century.

Also, it can be as simple as buying the motifs you love. For Widener, it’s country scenes and images of animals. He’s also not put off if they don’t have a frame at all.

“If you aren’t heading to Jaipur anytime soon, then Etsy and eBay are great places to look,” Holasz shares. “Sellers restock weekly. I’d browse quite a few listings before choosing, because the frame can make such a difference. They can sell pretty quicky as they are always in demand though, so it’s worth doing some research to shortlist styles you like so you can move quickly when the right thing comes along.”

If you’re searching for the truly antique varieties (made in the 18th and 19th centuries), Holasz adds that these are likely more available at auctions.

Also, don’t count out flea markets and antique shops for these finds. For reverse glass paintings, according to current eBay listings, you can expect to pay as low as $5 all the way up to $5,000. The prices for these paintings tend to be a steal these days since they’re just starting to have a moment, so snatch them up before the prices rise.

While you can certainly give the spotlight to a single reverse glass painting, many designers believe these diminutive pieces look best when hung together in small groups.

As for rooms where these works look their best, Holasz thinks they look “really adorable” in children’s rooms, from nurseries to bedrooms to playrooms. They also act as hardy artwork that can be displayed in kitchens and baths, since, as Lissack says, the paintings are “protected by being on the rear side of the glass.”

Also, don’t be afraid to go a bit rogue with these pieces. For example, Holasz likes to mix them into a gallery wall alongside oil paintings, drawings, and sketches, since the different surfaces create visual interest. And you don’t have to hang them on the wall—they also look great leaned on bookshelves.

Widener, who often scores unframed versions, prefers to display them on wire plate hangers.

“When placed in a window, the light comes through in a really beautiful way,” he says.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6902. A Complete Timeline of Taylor Swift and Karlie Kloss’ Friendship, Fallout, and Reunion

When it was first rumored that Taylor Swift’s ex-friend Karlie Kloss would be attending her wedding to Travis Kelce, I didn’t quite believe it. But then on July 3, there she was making her way to Madison Square Garden, dressed in gold and with her husband Josh Kushner in tow. And in case you thought that was a blip: The two were most recently spotted sitting side-by-side at a Chiefs game to cheer on Travis, so their friendship is, once again, under a full-on microscope.

And in case you’re new here—i.e. you weren’t obsessing over all things Karlie + Taylor in the mid-aughts—we have a timeline to get you caught up. In other words, find out how these two went from being best friends (they were featured on a Vogue cover together!), to pretty much estranged, to cordial (if not downright friendly) again. Starting with...

Taylor performs at the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show and has a moment with Karlie:

And Karlie just gushes, telling Hollywood Life, “We met at the show. We’d never met before but we’re BFFs now.”

Taylor and Karlie become pretty much instant best friends and go road-tripping together.

The BFFs are snapped while out and about together in NYC:

Then they chill at the Met Gala together.

Karlie and Taylor are spotted in NYC again!

Taylor tells Rolling Stone she has a guest room where “Karlie usually stays.”

And then they take in a game with...Ben Stiller?

They spend pretty much all of November chilling:

Taylor performs at the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show again, and Karlie documents the moment on Insta.

Soon after, fans become convinced Taylor and Karlie are dating (a conspiracy theory that begins with a blurry photo published by TMZ of the two at The 1975’s concert), even coining the couple’s name “Kaylor,” but Tay’s rep calls a story of them making out “crap.” More specifically, she tells Us Weekly, “It’s sad that on the day it’s announced Taylor has three Grammy nominations for ‘Shake It Off,’ I have to shake off this crap.”

A few days later, Tay tweets, “As my 25th birthday present from the media, I’d like for you to stop accusing all my friends of dating me. #thirsty.”

Then Taylor invites a fleet of other celebs—including Karlie—to celebrate her birthday:

Taylor and Karlie share a Vogue cover, which like...what best friends haven’t? (Obviously JK, this is a huge deal.)

Karlie appears in Taylor’s "Bad Blood" music video:

Karlie and the rest of the Squad™️ join Taylor on tour:

Karlie attends Taylor’s notorious July 4th party:

Amid Taylor’s feud with Kim Kardashian and Kanye West, Karlie is asked by the Sunday Times if she thinks Kim is a “good person” and says, “I think she’s been a lovely person to me in the past. Look, I really don’t know her that well.”

The next day, she takes to Twitter and writes, “I will not allow the media to misconstrue my words. Taylor has always had my back and I will always have hers.”

The pair celebrates Drake’s birthday together:

And then it’s Tay’s b-day, so Karlie goes ahead and celebrates on Instagram:

Karlie hangs with Kendall Jenner instead of Taylor for July 4th, which Swifties take very personally.

Taylor drops the video for “Look What You Made Me Do,” and fans immediately notice that Karlie’s name isn’t on a T-shirt that features other members of her Squad.

The Blast reports Taylor “politely declined” being on Karlie’s new show Movie Night With Karlie due to “scheduling issues.”

Amid rumors of tension between them, Karlie drops a HBD post to Taylor:

Karlie posts this video of herself dribbling a basketball on Instagram, which you might think means nothing:

But actually means everything because she initially captions it “swish swish”—a phrase Swifties interpret as being a direct reference to Katy Perry’s song of the same name, which is universally considered to be about Katy’s feud with Taylor.

Karlie is seen hanging with Katy Perry, causing Swifties to spiral even further. As in, people are straight-up leaving rat emojis on Karlie’s Insta.

Even Jennifer Lawrence asks the question on everyone’s mind during a TimesTalks:

The New York Times profiles Karlie and writes, “Her closest friends include Taylor Swift. They have vacationed together and have sat courtside at a New York Knicks game. Despite internet gossip suggesting otherwise, the pair continues to be good friends and they talk frequently. ‘Don’t believe everything you read,’ Ms. Kloss said.”

The following month, Karlie gives an interview with Porter shouting out all her best friends...and doesn’t mention Taylor.

“I’ve built really amazing friendships—some that last, some that don’t,” she says. “A couple have become some of the most important relationships in my life. In particular, those I’ve been in the trenches with, such as Jourdan Dunn, Joan Smalls, Toni Garrn, and Lily Aldridge. We started as girls and we've grown up together, and I know if I need anything, I can call them up and they will be there for me, just like my friends from kindergarten.”

Karlie shuts down feud rumors with a BTS post from Taylor’s Reputation World Tour:

Karlie films 73 Questions and is asked about Taylor, saying, “The world needs to know? Well, Jennifer Lawrence was interested. Jen, don’t worry, Taylor and I are still really good friends.”

Karlie gets married, and Taylor doesn’t show. Page Six reports that Karlie’s rep says Tay was “unable to attend due to prior commitments.”

Karlie is asked about Taylor on Watch What Happens Live (specifically about an essay Tay wrote about friendships for Elle), and says, “Taylor is incredible, and I feel really lucky to call her a friend, and she’s one of the hardest working women, and I loved her essay.”

Karlie appears on Scooter Braun’s Instagram, a huuuuuuuge yikes considering Scooter’s ongoing drama with Tay.

Taylor drops “It’s Time to Go,” and fans think these lyrics specifically are about Karlie. Ahem: “When the words of a sister come back in whispers / That prove she was not in fact what she seemed / Not a twin from your dreams / She’s a crook who was caught.”

Karlie shocks literally everyone by showing up at Taylor’s Eras Tour stop in Los Angeles—notably in the stands and not the VIP tent.

The Crazy Days and Nights blog drops a blind item reading, “I guess talking almost every day during the pregnancy brought the model and the singer close enough again to get the wedding invite.”

Soon after, TMZ reports that the former best friends have “officially buried the hatchet” and that “Taylor wants Karlie at her wedding.” The outlet also confirms there were “real issues between them,” but that they made up “quite some time ago” and it’s “all water under the bridge.”

And the rumors are true! Karlie shows up to Taylor’s wedding in gold, and fans are both shocked and delighted.

Fans are once again shook when Taylor rolls up to the November 8 Chiefs game to cheer on Travis with Karlie in tow.

#6900. All the Stars at the Harper’s Bazaar UK Women of the Year Awards

Including Mikey Madison, Cate Blanchett, and Isla Fisher

Here in the United States, Harper’s Bazaar hosts its annual Icons event, where this year’s honorees included none other than Céline Dion, Anna Sawai, Gabbriette, Charlotte Rampling, Cara Delevingne, Chris Fleming, Alysa Liu, Julie Mehretu, and Kim Gordon. But across the pond, our sister publication, Harper’s Bazaar UK, hosts its own awards ceremony, called Women of the Year, which celebrated its 2026 iteration last night at Claridge’s in London.

Like our “Icons” designation, the Women of the Year ceremony highlights figures who have inspired the world through art, entertainment, fashion, sports, and more. And the big winners of the evening included names like Oscar-winning actor Mikey Madison and All’s Fair star Glenn Close, the latter of whom was chosen as the Icon recipient for the WOTY ceremony.

Other prizewinners included Chicken Shop Date host Amelia Dimoldenberg, who was given the Innovator Award, while the co-CEO’s of the National Theatre, Kate Varah and Indhu Rubasingham, were honored with the Changemakers Award. Givenchy’s Sarah Burton was celebrated for her design, while Stephanie Cavalli was recognized for her modeling. Cate Blanchett, Sam Taylor-Johnson, Claudia Winkleman, Dina Asher-Smith, and Bella Maclean all walked home with statuettes as well.

Plus, a few other starry figures—like Isla Fisher, Elizabeth Debicki, and Letitia Wright—made surprise appearances.

Ahead, we round up all the celebrities who attended the 2026 Women of the Year Awards hosted by Harper’s Bazaar UK in London. Check out all the red-carpet photos below.

#6898. ‘Love Is Blind’ Divorces: Revisiting the 9 Couples Who Split After Marrying on the Show

The only couple to say “I do” during season 11 has already called it quits.

Love Is Blind season 9 made history as the first season in the show’s run to result in zero successful weddings—but IMO, that isn’t such a bad thing! While an impressive eight Love Is Blind couples are still happily married and (seemingly) thriving, another nine LIB marriages ended in divorce. The latest LIB alums to call it quits? Sarah Williams and John Shannon, who were the only couple to make it down the aisle on season 11 only to break up before the reunion.

Which other Love Is Blind couples have split, you ask? Now that season 11 has come to a close, let’s do a quick review of all the Love Is Blind divorces that have taken place during the show’s run.

Iyanna McNeely and Jarrette Jones (Season 2)

The first Love Is Blind couple to announce their divorce following the show was Iyanna and Jarrette, who tied the knot in June 2021 and decided to go their separate ways after just over a year of marriage.

The two shared the news with fans in a since-deleted Instagram post in August 2022, writing, “After much thought, we’re saddened to share that we have separated and will begin the process of divorcing. While we have love for each other, our lives are going in different directions, and that’s okay. Coming to this decision was far from easy and we will always wish each other the absolute best.”

Nick Thompson and Danielle Ruhl (Season 2)

Just about a week after Iyanna and Jarrette announced their breakup, the only other LIB season 2 couple to make it down the aisle, Nick and Danielle, also decided to call it quits.

Danielle filed for divorce in August 2022, later taking to her Instagram Stories to explain, “It really did come down to just personality compatibility differences. I think that’s what makes it even harder because you do live in this, at least for me, I kind of live in this state of denial where it’s like, ‘But we love each other.’ You can still love each other and still try your best to move on. It’s hard, it’s very hard.”

Tyler Frances and Ashley Adionser (Season 7)

One of the most controversial couples to come out of Love Is Blind, Tyler and Ashley married in November 2023 and announced their split just over a year later.

“After much reflection, I want to share that Tyler and I have been separated for several weeks and have now made the difficult decision to end our marriage,” Ashley told People in January 2025. “While I had hoped for mutual understanding and transparency in our relationship, it has become clear that our paths are no longer aligned, making it impossible for me to continue in this marriage.”

Fun fact: Seasons 2 and 3 of LIB were filmed at the same time, so Matt and Colleen also said “I do” in June 2021. And while their relationship lasted longer than both season 2 marriages, they ended up splitting after nearly four years together.

In a joint statement shared to Instagram in May 2025, the pair revealed, “We’ve decided to end our marriage and move forward on our own paths. We’ve been separated for some time now and have been doing our best to work through everything privately and with care.”

Milton Johnson and Lydia Arleen Velez-Gonzalez (Season 5)

The only successful couple to come out of LIB season 5, Milton and Lydia tied the knot in May 2022 only to end up calling it quits just about three years later.

Lydia revealed the news during a June 2025 appearance on the What’s the Reality? podcast, telling fellow LIB alum AD Smith, “It wasn’t one thing. It was a layer of situations and things that just kept piling up.”

Jordan Faeth and Amber Morrison (Season 10)

Jordan and Amber officially took the title of shortest LIB marriage when they announced at the season 10 reunion that they’d decided to split in August 2025 after just four months together. The problem: Neither was willing to relocate (Jordan wanted to stay in Columbus, while Amber had already built a home with her daughter in Mount Vernon), which led to distrust.

As Amber put it at the reunion, “When I pictured marriage, I pictured somebody to come home with after work, cooking, having a good time. If I’m with somebody I love, I don’t really care where I live. I’m not focusing on where my friends are, where the bars are. Why wouldn’t he want to be cuddled up with his wife at the end of the night?”

After getting married in June 2021 (and later welcoming a child together in July 2024), season 3’s Brennon filed for divorce from Alexa in November 2025.

Alexa didn’t share the news with fans until nearly a month later, writing on Instagram, “After much reflection and many heartfelt conversations, we have made the difficult decision to end our marriage. This choice was not made lightly, and it comes with a great deal of care, respect, and appreciation for the time we’ve shared.”

Sarah Williams and John Shannon (Season 11)

The Boston-based pair was the only couple from their season to tie the knot, saying “I do” back in March 2025. However, it was later revealed at the reunion that they’d decided to separate after just over a year together.

According to John, the split was due to him sending “inappropriate” texts to an escort who’d given him her number on a boys’ night out. “I crossed a line, a boundary that she had set down, and I don’t blame her at all,” he said of Sarah ending the marriage.

Chelsea Griffin and Kwame Appiah (Season 4)

Though it took some convincing to get Kwame to leave Portland for Seattle, he eventually did, and he and Chelsea were married for four years before they announced their decision to divorce.

In separate statements released on Instagram in May 2026, Chelsea explained that she and Kwame were “growing in different directions,” while Kwame said that their “ultimate life goals don’t feel aligned.”
