#6892. 8 Angelic Powdery Perfumes That Smell Like Heaven on Earth

These aren’t your grandmother's powder perfumes, FYI.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Once upon a time, powdery perfumes only smelled like powder cosmetic compacts with overwhelming hints of violet, orris, rose, and musk. The original powder-forward scents probably reminded you of your grandmother's lipstick or her signature floral scent (no offense, Nana). The classic fragrance family has gotten a bit of a makeover, with a whole new vibe that’s fluffy, heavenly, and fit for any age.

Today's powdery perfumes are complex, thanks to the addition of notes like vanilla, amber, and sandalwood, says perfumer Bérénice Watteau. "Many powdery perfumes have a velvety and enveloping feel on the skin, making them a great fit for anybody who is looking for a warm, creamy fragrance,” she explains. If this type of scent sounds appealing, then you're in the right place, because team Cosmo rounded up the best powdery perfumes. We've personally sniffed, spritzed, and fallen in love with each one, and can confidently say there's a perfume on the list for everyone.

Keep scrolling for the best powdery perfumes that will make you feel like a sophisticated angel. 👼

One Cosmo editor says: “Honey perfumes are difficult to get right. Many can smell too sticky or syrupy. Turns out, a swirl of powder—in the form of heliotrope, coconut, and iris—was all that was needed to make the note live up to its liquid gold reputation. I feel like I’ve jumped tax brackets when I spritz this scent. (I’m manifesting!) It’s delicately sweet and airy, and lasts forever. Seriously, all I need is one spritz, and I can still smell it on myself after 12 hours.”—MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: "I always thought powdery perfumes would make me smell like a grandma, but either I'm inching toward grandma status or Angel Dust by Fugazzi is built different. It's powdery but not from heavy florals. Instead, the scent combines a mix of skin-like cashmere, musk, and amber. It's soft and reminiscent of the inside of a knitwear shop.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: “Have you ever wondered what the clouds smell like? Well, scientists confirmed the aroma is fluffy, powdery, soft, and sparkling. Future Society stuck a petri dish out of a plane to capture cloud microbes to create a photo-realistic perfume inspired by the clouds. (I love science!) This carries an airy aura as sweet sugar, sparkling aldehydes, and aquatic blooms tickle my nose. The dry down feels atmospheric and clean thanks to delicate musks.”—MH

One Cosmo editor says: “In my experience, powdery perfumes are some of the most polarizing. (People either love or hate them.) Everyone seems to adore Guerlain’s take on powder when I wear L’Heure Bleue, though. Every time I mist this ethereal perfume, the compliments roll in. The ozonic-oceanic notes ripple like waves as they mingle with hints of powdery iris and the most delicate vanilla. Thank goodness the price is relatively affordable compared to other scents from the luxury house because I’ve nearly finished my bottle in three months.”—MH

One Cosmo editor says: "Glossier You Fleur is hands-down my fave fragrance from the brand. If Gen Z was a powder perfume, it would be this. Yes, it's floral, but not in a matronly rose way. It boasts the same scent composition as the OG Glossier You, but it also has ylang-ylang and osmanthus to sweeten it. Plus, notes of ambrox and cashmeran ground it with a warm and modern powdery vibe."—JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: “This is the most powdery perfume I own, to the point that it honestly smells like baby powder—if there was such a thing as expensive baby powder for rich lil nepo babies. It's not your standard rose-y, mature powder perfume. Instead, there's clove, plum, vanilla, and cashmere that give it a sensual musky aura."—BG

One Cosmo editor says: "Chanel is basically synonymous with powder perfumes, and Chance eau Tendre is Chanel’s most citrusy, fresh scent, per our testing. But don’t let the fruitiness fool you—this could so easily be worn year-round, for either day or night. The added musk and rose really balances it all out and leaves you smelling like you just took the best everything shower."—BG

One Cosmo editor says: "Prada Paradoxe is the epitome of a sexy and juicy perfume that has a surprising powdery finish to it. If you want to smell flirty, floral, and fruity without being overly sweet, I could not recommend this scent more. It opens with notes of tangerine, pear, and jasmine, then it dries down with amber and musk to leave behind a warm and skin-like powdery aroma. It's my favorite nighttime fragrance when I want to smell straight up irresistible."—JH

Watteau says that just like adding a touch of sea salt to chocolate to bring out its flavor, choosing a fragrance with contrasting notes will help powder perfumes really shine. “Powdery notes are fluffy, enveloping a super soft and comforting feeling if they’re well balanced. Without this element of contrast it can become dense and heavy or dry and suffocating,” she says. She also recommends going for something with a complementary green, citrus, or woody note to allow the fragrance to “breathe” and become more dimensional.

Powdery perfumes have evolved in recent years and because of this, the category has expanded in surprising ways. That means if you like florals, earthy scents, spicy fragrances, or even deep, more mysterious aromas, you'll be able to find a powdery perfume for your vibe. I recommend shopping by your your primary fragrance category first, and then look for words like "soft" "smooth" or "powdery" in the description.

Mary Honkusis a beauty contributor for Cosmopolitan with over eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing beauty stories. She is an authority in all beauty categories, but has a sweet spot for fragrance with a collection of over 1,000 scents. For this story, she trialed dozens of powdery perfumes and asked Cosmo editors for their fluffy, otherworldly picks.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmopolitan who writes about all things fragrance. For this story, she tapped her expansive fragrance collection and other Cosmo editors to find the best powdery perfumes.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6888. Taylor Swift and Karlie Kloss Are Hanging Out Again—And Fans Think Taylor Soft-Launched It With Easter Eggs

An investigation for those still at the restaurant.

In case you needed a sign that the blue wave is blue waving after the midterm elections’ Democratic sweep, Taylor Swift and Karlie Kloss are hanging out again like it’s 2016.

The pair were spotted in a box together during the Kansas City Chiefs game against the New York Jets on Sunday, November 8, and based on their smiles and hugs, it seems like no time has passed between them.

Naturally, longtime Swifties lost any ability to chill when it came to their reunion—especially since they had seemingly kept their distance for a few years.

“When they said woke 3.0 was coming, I didn’t think that would mean seeing Taylor Swift and Karlie Kloss together again,” one person wrote, while another added, “That wasn’t on my 2026 bingo card.”

This also means that Swifties are back to theorizing about Taylor and Karlie’s connection. Some even suggested the Grammy winner soft-launched their game-day hangout with a series of Easter eggs while talking about her Oscar contender for Best Original Song, “I Knew It, I Knew You.”

“‘I Knew It, I Knew You’ is about Karlie and I’ll die on that hill,” one fan theorized, as another asked, “Wondering if Taylor wrote ‘I Knew It, I Knew You’ about rekindling her friendship with Karlie...?” alongside a clip of Taylor discussing the song during the Toy Story 5 premiere last summer.

“I think when people hear the song ‘I Knew It, I Knew You’ at the end of Toy Story 5, I think it would be really awesome if they thought of somebody that they had amazing memories with that they haven’t seen in a long time, or someone that they miss, knowing that that person could come back into their life,” Taylor explained during the film’s premiere.

She added, “Or just the feeling that, like, sometimes people’s paths diverge and that doesn’t mean that that’s the last time you get to feel those memories or know that person. Sometimes when you look back on your childhood, those memories, that’s special in itself.”

In case you need a refresher on why the internet is losing its mind over a 2026 Kaylor reunion, the girls became inseparable after they hit it off in 2012, from walking the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show to gracing the cover of Vogue together.

Fans eventually speculated that Taylor and Karlie had a falling out after the model married venture capitalist Josh Kushner in 2018, though it had never been confirmed. (Josh’s brother, Jared, married President Donald Trump’s daughter, Ivanka, in 2009.)

However, Karlie and Josh both attended Taylor’s wedding to Travis in July, so…it seems like there hasn’t been any bad blood for a minute.

#6882. Casio’s New Mechanical Field Watch Is a Game-Changer

The affordable titanium timepiece puts the Japanese brand squarely in the consideration set for budding collectors.

Since forever, Casio has dominated the market for affordable digital and quartz watches. Now it's making a play for the wallet-friendly end of the mechanical watch market, territory traditionally dominated by fellow Japanese manufacturers Seiko, Citizen, and Orient.

The new CMK-100 is Casio's first automatic watch to carry its mainline branding, following the introduction of mechanical models under its Edifice label last year. And on paper, it's a pretty compelling proposition.

Inspired by classic military field watches—with more than a passing resemblance to the Rolex Explorer and the Tudor Ranger—the CMK-100 has a 38mm titanium case, sapphire crystal, screw-down crown, and 100 meters of water resistance. Its textured dial features luminous Arabic numerals, while a Japanese automatic movement provides a 42-hour power reserve. The movement's manufacturer hasn't been disclosed, although its specifications suggest it could be a Miyota 8215, a calibre used by Timex, Spinnaker, and Invicta, among others.

There are five versions: one on a titanium bracelet, two on nylon straps, and two more on Italian leather straps—including a rather jazzy brown-dial model with a rose gold-colored case. Prices range from $330 to $440.

For Seiko, which has built a substantial business selling affordable mechanical watches through collections such as the Seiko 5 Sports, Casio's arrival in this category represents an interesting challenge. Titanium cases and sapphire crystals are hardly standard equipment at this price, and Casio already has an enormous following among younger watch buyers.

Whether those customers will embrace a mechanical from the home of G-Shock remains to be seen. But for anyone shopping for a good-looking, affordable automatic watch, there's now another Japanese name in the running for your attention.

#6877. Seiko’s Affordable Hideo Kojima Watches Are a Gamer's Dream—but There's a Catch

The Japanese brand has teamed up with Kojima Productions to celebrate its 10th anniversary with two limited-edition timepieces.

If you're a fan of Hideo Kojima's video games, you might want to take a look at Seiko's later collaboration. The watchmaker has teamed up with Kojima Productions, the studio behind Death Stranding, to release two limited-edition watches celebrating its 10th anniversary, both exclusive to Japan.

And, in keeping with Kojima's rather distinctive creative universe, these aren't your typical Seikos.

The new Ludens watches come in two colourways, black and white, with just 300 of each available. Both take inspiration from Ludens, the futuristic, spacesuit-wearing mascot of Kojima Productions, whose illustration takes pride and place on the dial alongside the studio's motto From Sapiens to Ludens.

The white edition features a silver-and-white dial paired with a patterned white strap, while the black version offers a darker alternative. Turn either watch over and you'll find an engraved illustration of Ludens on the case back, alongside the Seiko logo and an individual serial number.

As for what's inside, both watches are powered by Seiko's battery-operated 6N01 quartz movement, which offers an accuracy of approximately 15 seconds per month and a battery life of around three years. There's also a stainless steel case, Hardlex crystal, and everyday water resistance.

The black edition is priced at ¥55,000 (approximately $350), while the white version costs ¥57,600 (around $365). The latter also comes with a ticket to Kojima Productions' Beyond the Strand 10th-anniversary exhibition, taking place at Tokyo's Azabudai Hills Gallery until December 20.

Pre-orders opened in Japan on October 30 through Lawson and HMV&BOOKS, with reservations running until December 14, unless all 300 pieces sell out first.

But here's the catch. Despite being available to pre-order now, neither watch is expected to arrive until September 30 2027. That's almost eleven months of waiting for a watch that's already rather difficult to get hold of, particularly if you're shopping from outside Japan.

Still, for diehard Kojima fans, a limited-edition Seiko celebrating a decade of one of gaming's most recognizable studios might just be worth the wait.

#6876. Why Is Everyone Bragging About Their Ferritin Levels?

Losing hair, feeling sluggish, or having brain fog? Experts are turning to a blood-work statistic that may be the sneaky cause

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

“Maybe you’re not depressed and unmotivated … maybe you just have low ferritin,” says Elizabeth Orringo, a wellness influencer and host of The Wellness Process podcast, in an Instagram reel. The caption then prompts viewers to comment “Iron” to get a link to her Substack, which details how she raised her ferritin from 16 to 142, making her body “feel like a new person.” The video has 244,000 views and nearly 5,000 “Iron” comments. But what is ferritin, how does it relate to iron, and why do thousands of women online care?

Ferritin and iron aren’t synonymous, but they are closely linked. “Ferritin is a protein that stores iron in your body, and iron is essential for producing hemoglobin, the protein in red blood cells responsible for carrying oxygen throughout the body,” says Pooja Gidwani, a Los Angeles–based internist and longevity expert. “Ferritin is one of the best markers we have to assess how much iron your body has stored, rather than just how much is circulating in your blood at a given time.”

Iron has had its moment in the wellness world, but ferritin is just getting started; it stores iron for later use, so the less you have, the less iron supply your body has in times of need, like during increased blood loss, growth stages, and life in general. “I like to think of serum iron as your checking account and ferritin as your savings account,” says Amanda Kahn, a New York–based internist and longevity specialist. “You can have enough money to cover today’s expenses while your savings are becoming depleted.” While iron gets most of the spotlight, Kahn says ferritin often gives a clearer picture of the body’s iron reserves and can more efficiently identify iron deficiency before it progresses to anemia and other problems.

Even without a blood test, people often notice iron deficiency through low energy, hair loss, and fatigue, which can also be side effects of low ferritin. But another marker of low ferritin that has taken social media by storm is “ferritin face.”

“While ‘ferritin face’ isn’t a recognized medical condition, the broader connection between iron status, energy, and overall well-being is worth understanding,” Kahn explains. “Social media has introduced terms like ‘ferritin face,’ bringing attention to the relationship between iron stores, energy, and appearance.”

According to Google Search, searches for “ferritin face” are up 450 percent in the last three months and 5,000 percent year over year. Ferritin face refers to the more visible hallmarks of low ferritin, like pale skin, dark, hollow under-eyes due to lack of color, and puffiness as a result of inflammation. In a beauty landscape where going under the knife is often the first line of defense, “ferritin face” offers another explanation that plastic surgery can’t fix. “When iron is in short supply, the body deprioritizes skin and hair and redirects it to support vital organs, which is why it shows up visibly first,” says Tiffany Lester, an integrative medicine physician and women’s health medical director at Function.

Just because you have dark circles or look pale doesn’t mean you have low ferritin—and vice versa; someone without “ferritin face” can still have low ferritin. The first step is a blood-work panel that covers both iron and ferritin levels, since iron levels can be normal while ferritin can be low. “If your iron levels read normal, but you have low ferritin, which means your body may still have enough circulating iron to meet its immediate needs, but its stored iron is depleted,” says Gidwani.

However, experts point out that laboratories often have different ideal ranges for ferritin levels, and a “healthy” ferritin level can be hard to pinpoint. “Reference ranges reflect a population average, not your optimal baseline,” Lester explains. This means that an adequate level for you may not be normal for someone else. “A lab may tell a woman that a ferritin of 16 nanograms per liter is ‘normal,’ while another woman has a ferritin of 100 nanograms per liter and is also considered normal. I often ask, ‘How could women possibly feel the same across such a wide range?’” says Kahn.

Doctors agree that ferritin below 15 nanograms per milliliter and iron below 30 nanograms per milliliter indicate a deficiency. However, Kahn points out that ferritin levels that are too high aren’t good either. “I also pay close attention to women with ferritin between 30 and 50 nanograms per liter, particularly when they’re experiencing fatigue, exercise intolerance, or hair shedding,” she says. This isn’t to say levels in the 100s are unhealthy. “Reference ranges reflect a population average, not your optimal baseline,” Lester adds.

There is no one-size-fits-all approach to improving your ferritin levels, but you can follow the same protocol you’d use to increase iron and ferritin, since more iron means more for ferritin to store. You can’t pinpoint the causes of the deficiency, but the best place to start is diet. “Diet is often assumed to be the cause, but menstrual blood loss, gastrointestinal blood loss, and impaired absorption are important considerations,” says Gidwani. Historically, the best food sources of iron have been animal sources like red meat, which contains heme iron, generally absorbed more efficiently than the nonheme iron found in plant foods like lentils, beans, and spinach, Gidwani adds. “Pairing plant-based iron sources with vitamin C, such as citrus fruits or bell peppers, can improve absorption.”

Habits are important too, Lester explains. Your daily coffee or tea with breakfast may be affecting your iron and ferritin levels. “Caffeine blocks gut absorption of the iron-rich foods we are consuming,” she adds. Consider having your morning caffeine an hour or two before or after you eat.

The same goes for supplements—experts say to avoid drinking caffeine when you take an iron supplement. “Oral iron is usually the first treatment, and alternate-day dosing can improve absorption and sometimes tolerability,” Gidwani says. “IV iron may be appropriate when oral iron isn’t tolerated, isn’t effective, or absorption is impaired.” Experts also recommend pairing an iron supplement with a vitamin C supplement, similar to the aforementioned food pairings, because vitamin C helps iron absorption.

Nature Made® Iron 18 mg Gummies with Vitamin C

If ferritin remains low despite consistent supplementation, it may be worth looking into why the iron isn’t being absorbed. Popular culprits are gut conditions like celiac disease, autoimmune gastritis, SIBO, and IBS, as they all interfere with iron absorption. Even poor stomach acid can affect absorption.

Bragging about your TikTok followers may not be the most socially appropriate thing, but bragging about your ferritin levels certainly is. However, more isn’t always better. “I always remind my patients that we need to look at the entire physiological picture, and there is also a meaningful difference between treating a genuine deficiency and simply trying to push a laboratory value higher,” Kahn emphasizes.

“My philosophy is that laboratory values should be interpreted in the context of the individual, not just whether a number falls inside a ‘normal’ reference range,” she adds. “The goal isn’t to chase a perfect ferritin number. It’s to identify deficiencies, understand why they’re occurring, and help women feel and function their best.”

#6871. Why You’re Still Getting Acne in Your 20s, 30s, and 40s, According to Dermatologists

Acne experts break down adult acne and how to treat it.

Throughout my teenage years, I was blessedly acne-free. I experienced severe eczema, and for that, I thought the trade-off for my dryness and rashes was the rarity of a pimple. Then I turned 22 and my face became a hotbed for breakouts, so much so that I started taking oral spironolactone and underwent an entire skincare program with Face Reality to get everything under control.

Now I’m 26, and while I am off the daily meds and have the ability to be more liberal with my regimen, I still experience breakouts across my chin from time to time. When I spoke with friends and found out so many of them were also experiencing acne for the first time and going on Accutane and spironolactone, I concluded that adult acne is not nearly as addressed as teenage acne.

Of course, for teenagers, breakouts can be difficult and stressful, but they’re common, and skincare brands like Starface and Hero Cosmetics have helped normalize pimple patches and mitigate the insecurity of having acne. But for adults in their 20s, 30s, and 40s who also experience breakouts, acne is far less discussed.

“I think one of the biggest misconceptions about acne is that it's mostly a teenage skin condition,” says Stephanie Criscione, licensed aesthetician and senior director of education at Face Reality. “Acne can show up at almost any stage of life, and someone who’s 35 may need a very different approach than someone who’s 15, even if their breakouts look similar.”

Below, I chatted with board-certified dermatologist Karan Lal, MD, and licensed aestheticians Samantha Susca and Criscione for all their insight on the different phases of acne and what treatment might look like throughout every age.

“Teenage acne is often driven by the hormonal surge associated with puberty, which increases oil production and contributes to clogged follicles and inflammation,” Criscione explains. “We tend to see a combination of blackheads, whiteheads, and inflammatory lesions, often affecting larger areas of the face and sometimes the chest and back.”

Susca adds, “the increase of certain androgen production and sebaceous activity, coupled with the lifestyle of a teenager, which is often not the most hygienic or knowledgeable about personal care,” can contribute to breakouts.

Dr. Lal adds that teenage acne “tends to involve a broader distribution across the face, particularly the T-zone, and can also affect the chest and back.”

Unfortunately, leaving your teenage years doesn’t always mean leaving acne behind. Our experts all agree that adult acne can be more complicated to treat because there isn’t a one-size-fits-all solution, and the root causes can vary. “Acne that arises in your 20s and 30s is more likely to be due to hormonal fluctuations, which are not necessarily always hormonal imbalances,” Susca explains.

“Fluctuations can occur from things like age and stress, pregnancies, stopping or starting contraceptives or other medications, as well as genetic and family history of perimenopause, which can happen as early as in the 30s age range,” Susca says. “Inflammation paired with stress, lifestyle, travel, and using an incorrect routine can be factors that contribute as well.”

In terms of what these breakouts actually look like, “We tend to see more persistent inflammatory acne in some adults, particularly around the lower face,” says Criscione. This includes the jawline and the chin, which are areas most commonly associated with hormonal acne.

Dr. Lal adds: “These breakouts can be persistent, cyclical, and more likely to leave behind post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation or scarring.”

And something that often gets overlooked is that adult skin has competing needs, which can further compromise the skin barrier and contribute to persistent acne. “Someone in their 30s may be dealing with breakouts while simultaneously experiencing dehydration, sensitivity, pigmentation, or early signs of aging,” Criscione says. “Unfortunately, reaching for the strongest acne treatment on the shelf can end up compromising the skin barrier and making those other concerns worse.” So it’s important to be selective about the products addressing other skin concerns if you’re also dealing with acne at this stage.

“Unfortunately, turning 40 or 50 doesn’t automatically mean we’re in the clear, pun intended,” Criscione says. Hormonal changes can continue to influence the skin well into midlife, and acne can take on a different appearance during this stage. “Perimenopausal and menopausal acne is often very red and inflamed, presenting as cystic in many cases,” Susca explains.

“As estrogen levels fluctuate and eventually decline, the relative influence of androgens on the sebaceous glands may become more pronounced,” says Dr. Lal. “Some patients experience new or worsening acne despite having had relatively clear skin for years.”

“In menopause, the skin also often becomes drier, thinner, and more sensitive,” explains Dr. Lal. “This creates an interesting clinical challenge, where patients may simultaneously experience acne and reduced skin barrier function, so treatment must address breakouts without unnecessarily compromising hydration or skin integrity.”

According to Dr. Lal, acne treatment should be individualized based on several factors, including age, acne severity, skin type, hormonal status, and risk of scarring.

For teenagers, treatment often starts by addressing excess oil and clogged pores. “In teenagers, I often focus on establishing a simple, consistent routine,” explains Dr. Lal. Classic acne-fighting ingredients like “benzoyl peroxide can be particularly helpful for inflammatory acne, and salicylic acid may be useful for patients with predominantly clogged pores. More severe cases may require prescription therapies,” he adds.

“In the 20s and 30s age range, treatment often needs to address both acne and the patient’s concerns about pigmentation, texture, and early photoaging,” says Dr. Lal. “Topical retinoids are valuable, while azelaic acid can be an excellent option for patients who experience both acne and post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation.” In some cases for women, he adds that hormonal prescriptions such as spironolactone or certain combined oral contraceptives may be beneficial.

“For patients in their 40s and beyond, I place greater emphasis on balancing efficacy with tolerability,” says Dr. Lal. “The skin may be drier or more sensitive, so I may recommend a lower-strength retinoid, less frequent application, or a more supportive moisturizing regimen.”

Dr. Lal also emphasizes that treatment should account for the specific type of acne someone is experiencing. “For comedonal acne, retinoids are particularly important; for inflammatory acne, benzoyl peroxide and prescription anti-inflammatory treatments may be needed, and for deep nodular or scarring acne, systemic treatment—including isotretinoin in appropriate patients—may be necessary.”

“Androgens, which are present in both men and women, stimulate oil production in the skin,” says Dr. Lal. “During puberty, that increase in oil can contribute to clogged follicles and inflammatory breakouts, and as we move into adulthood, fluctuations associated with menstrual cycles, pregnancy, and perimenopause can influence oil production and inflammation, sometimes triggering acne in people who haven't experienced breakouts in years.”

“Acne is not simply a teenage problem,” Dr. Lal assures. “It is a condition that can change in its presentation and treatment needs throughout adulthood.”

There are several different types of acne, ranging from clogged pores to deeper, more painful inflammatory breakouts. Here’s how to tell them apart.

Comedonal acne: “Comedonal acne is generally what we think of as blackheads and whiteheads,” explains Criscione. “These are caused by excess oil and dead skin cells.” Blackheads are open comedones, while whiteheads are closed comedones. Susca adds that comedonal acne typically “looks like skin-colored bumps under the skin and it’s often associated with hormonal acne in teenagers and in early 20s.”

Inflammatory acne: “This includes papules and pustules, which can appear as red, raised bumps or lesions containing visible pus,” says Dr. Lal. “inflammatory breakouts can sometimes leave behind pigmentation or scarring.” Criscione adds that “More severe inflammatory acne can involve deeper nodules or cysts that are painful, persistent, appear like hardening lumps under the skin.”

Nodular or cystic acne: Susca describes this category of acne as “deep, hardened, painful inflammatory lesions beneath the skin, often with a higher risk of scarring or pigmentation.” Dr. Lal says “the term ‘cystic acne’ is commonly used by patients, although many deep lesions are technically inflammatory nodules rather than true cysts.”

Hormonal acne: Susca explains that “hormonal acne isn’t actually a separate lesion type, it's a description of acne influenced by hormonal activity, and it can present as anything from clogged pores to deeper inflammatory breakouts.”

“Hormonal acne is often associated with breakouts along the chin, jawline, and lower cheeks, particularly when breakouts are deep, tender, and tend to appear around the same time each month,” Criscione says.

If you’re experiencing adult acne at any stage of life, know that you’re not alone. Millions of people experience breakouts well beyond their teenage years, and acne can look different as your skin and hormones change over time.

Consider speaking with a skincare professional who can help identify what’s contributing to your breakouts and recommend the right ingredients, products, and treatments for your skin. Adult acne is common, treatable, and, most importantly, nothing to be embarrassed about!

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things skincare and has dealt with acne for the last five years. For this story, she consulted acne experts for all their insight on addressing and treating acne at all stages of life.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6866. Benzoyl Peroxide Is the Unsung Hero of Acne Treatments, So Why Is It So Controversial?

Derms and aestheticians agree...but very few brands have caught on.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In the early aughts, the options for acne treatments were limited but surprisingly effective. When I got my first breakout, my mom dragged me to CVS, where I stocked up on salicylic acid washes, witch hazel toners, apricot scrubs (IYKYK), and benzoyl peroxide spot treatments. When all of that didn’t work, we moved on to Proactiv, the three-step infomercial darling allegedly loved by the likes of Kelly Clarkson and Jessica Simpson. It also featured a benzoyl peroxide–spiked wash and spot treatment plus an exfoliating toner. Back then, curing your acne was fairly simple. Kill bacteria with benzoyl peroxide, then exfoliate with salicylic acid. But as skincare has advanced, and consumers have wised up to what ingredients actually do, we left benzoyl peroxide by the wayside. Why?

Earlier this year, still dealing with acne as I approached 30, I got a facial with aesthetician and self-proclaimed acne whisperer Sofie Pavitt. Her recommendation was simple: Wash four nights a week with a benzoyl peroxide cleanser (any drugstore option was fine, she told me), and pat on a mandelic acid serum every morning (she likes her own brand, of course). It sounded too good to be true. I’ve had hormonal acne since I was 11—something so simple couldn’t be the fix. But 10 months later, my skin has never been clearer, brighter, or more even.

I almost entirely credit my lack of breakouts to benzoyl peroxide, which if you didn’t know, helps kill acne-causing bacteria on the surface of your skin. I’ll shout it from the rooftops and to anyone who will listen that benzoyl peroxide cured my acne. But as I scroll through my inbox at every new acne launch, I’m confused why so few brands ever launch any products with the magic ingredient. I can count on one hand, literally, how many products have come out this year highlighting benzoyl peroxide. The list is vast for the other famed acne treatments: salicylic acid, tea tree oil, azelaic acid, niacinamide, sulfur, glycolic acid, and lactic acid.

But “dermatologists have never, never left,” dermatologist Heather Woolery-Lloyd, MD, tells me. “We always loved benzoyl peroxide because it works so well and is predictable,” she says. “It’s a hero when it comes to acne.” So where the heck did it go? And why is no one talking about it?

To understand benzoyl peroxide, you have to first understand how acne works. When you have acne-prone skin, your pores get very sticky with oil. You get a buildup of oil and bacteria in that pore, which is what turns into pimples. Benzoyl peroxide kills the acne-causing bacteria in that pore by releasing oxygen into the pore, breaking apart the bacteria, explains Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. It’s also a comedolytic, which, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd explains, means it can gently break down dead skin cells to reduce clogged pores.

First of all, it’s pretty difficult to use in formulating, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd explains. “It’s not the easiest thing to just throw into any lotion and know that it will be cosmetically elegant to apply to skin,” she says. “Dermatologists might think of it as a hero, but from a cosmetic scientist’s standpoint, it is a little more complicated to work with. It’s easier to use salicylic acid.”

But there’s also some red tape. Both benzoyl peroxide and salicylic acid are considered “over-the-counter drugs,” hence why there are drug facts on the back of the packaging. This just means that brands have to prove that they’re stable through independent testing. It’s good for the consumer, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd says, because then you know the product has the right percentage and that percentage is stable until its expiration date. But ingredients like niacinamide and azelaic acid can be added without the same regulatory burden. “It’s much easier to throw niacinamide into a product than add benzoyl peroxide,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd.

Maybe it’s my bias here, but I sense a benzoyl peroxide–aissance will be upon us shortly. Sofie Pavitt’s eponymous skincare line features the only benzoyl peroxide product at Sephora: a 5 percent spot treatment called Reset. She reformulated it from an allover face mask and relaunched this year, and it’s been one of my go-to spot treatments in 2026. But until the chic Sephora brands catch up, the drugstore is holding it down on the BP front. There’s PanOxyl, which is known for its 4 percent and 10 percent benzoyl peroxide cleansers (that’s what I use, FYI), as well as a new 10 percent spot treatment. CeraVe also offers a creamy 4 percent benzoyl peroxide cleanser with niacinamide and hyaluronic acid. An oldie but a goodie is Clean & Clear’s Persa-Gel 10, a 10 percent BP spot treatment gel that layers well under makeup. If that’s a bit too harsh for your skin, try La Roche-Posay’s 5.5 percent BP spot treatment.

Sofie Pavitt Face 5% Reset Benzoyl Peroxide Acne Treatment

PanOxyl Acne Creamy Wash, 4% Benzoyl Peroxide

Clean & Clear Persa-Gel 10 Acne Spot Treatment

La Roche-Posay Effaclar BPO Multi-Target Acne Treatment

Recently, prescription acne-treatment Epiduo, which combines 0.1 percent adapalene (a retinoid for acne) with 2.5 percent benzoyl peroxide, became available over the counter, which is guaranteed to be a game changer. “It’s really exciting for us because it is the most effective over-the-counter acne treatment now,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. “A few months ago, I’d have to write a prescription, and now you can walk into your local Walmart, Target, CVS, or Walgreens or even go on Amazon and buy it over the counter,” she says.

The formula rose to popularity because it has a unique delivery base, which “evenly suspends both of the active ingredients,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. “This prevents hot spots where you get local irritation.” Both retinoids and benzoyl peroxide can be drying, which is why they typically aren’t advised to be used together (and you shouldn’t try to cocktail it at home), but this gel base helps. You also only have to use it once per day, which Dr. Woolery-Lloyd loves since she knows that consistency is the key to all acne treatments.

Neither is necessarily better than the other, because they do different things. Benzoyl peroxide kills acne-causing bacteria on the surface of your skin, reducing the likelihood that bacteria can clog and inflame your pores. Salicylic acid, on the other hand, is an oil-soluble exfoliant. It goes into your pores and helps clear out the dead skin and oils that clog them. Both can be beneficial in an acne regimen, to be fair.

But it’s hard not to play favorites, for both me and Dr. Woolery-Lloyd. “When it comes to acne, benzoyl peroxide is my preferred ingredient for sure,” she says. And I agree. It’s the winner for my oily skin and persistent hormonal acne.

As much as I love benzoyl peroxide, I understand it can be drying, especially for those with already dry skin. To cut down on irritation and dryness, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd recommends opting for a lower concentration. “Benzoyl peroxide comes in many different concentrations over the counter, but the studies show that you don't really have to use those higher concentrations to get the efficacy,” she explains. The sweet spot is between 2.5 and 5 percent; you really don’t need to go with a 10 percent formula to get the benefits.

As far as the rest of your skincare routine with benzoyl peroxide, Dr. Woolery-Lloyd suggests a pH-balanced cleanser (on the days you don’t use a benzoyl peroxide formula) and moisturizer to help keep your skin barrier healthy. You can actually use moisturizer before or after your benzoyl peroxide formula (especially if you’re using something like Epiduo, which also contains a retinoid). “I personally always put medicine first and the moisturizer on top, but it actually doesn't matter as long as you're using a moisturizer that can help to minimize that risk of irritation,” says Dr. Woolery-Lloyd.

To improve tolerability, you can also start with every other day, or every third day, then increase if your skin reacts well. You can also try using a wash only once or twice a week. That’s what I do, and I opt for a hydrating cleanser on the off nights with lots of hydration and barrier care to offset any irritation, and my skin has handled it well. In fact, my skin looks the best it ever has right now. Thank you, benzoyl peroxide!

Heather Woolery-Lloyd, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist based in Miami. She is currently the director of the Skin of Color Division for the University of Miami Department of Dermatology.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan, where she covers skincare, makeup, hair, nails, and more across digital and print. She can generally be found in bright eyeshadow furiously typing her latest feature or hemming and hawing about a new product you "have to try." Prior to Cosmopolitan, she wrote and edited beauty content as an Editor at The Everygirl for four years. Follow her on Instagram for makeup selfies and a new hair 'do every few months.

#6865. Stylist Soki Mak on 10 Things That Inspired Kim Kardashian’s All’s Fair Season Two Wardrobe

From 1990s Helmut Newton shoots to classic Bond girls to unexpected Comme des Garçons finds

In fashion, anything from niche archival research to a favorite film to an outfit you saw on your morning commute can make for inspiration. In our new series 10 Things, we ask industry professionals and tastemakers to divulge the (you guessed it) 10 things that had the most impact on their aesthetic choices.

The second season of Ryan Murphy’s legal drama, All’s Fair, opened with reality star-turned-actor Kim Kardashian in a patchwork Alexander McQueen suit. The rare ensemble, composed of a peplum jacket and fluted skirt, came from Lee McQueen’s theatrical Spring 2004 collection, titled Deliverance, in which models danced down the runway in shimmering gowns, dramatic feathered skirts, and tulle galore. “There were comments about Kim’s clothing being inappropriate in the workplace, but that’s what it should be,” Soki Mak, the visionary behind Kardashian’s Allura Grant character, told Harper’s Bazaar. “If I’m going to watch a show, I want to be like, ‘What the hell is that?’”

When Mak first joined the All’s Fair team—handpicked by Kardashian, via Instagram, to dress her onscreen as a high-powered lawyer—she had never ventured into costume design. The stylist, who has worked with everyone from Rosalía to Pamela Anderson, spent the first season developing the visual identity of Allura, merging her client’s own flair for shock value and fantasy with the limited character descriptions she received. We saw hyper-cinched waists and archival Jean Paul Gaultier; Hermès Birkins and suits with exposed thongs. It was Kardashian, with a twist.

But for Season Two, Mak focused on separating Kardashian from her character, tracking Allura’s emotional journey through darker color palettes and less expected silhouettes. She dug deeper into designer archives, emerging with moody gems from some of the most beloved collections of the ’90s and early-aughts. Rare vintage pieces from Maison Margiela, Comme des Garçons, Yohji Yamamoto, and Alaïa each made their cameos.

Ahead, the stylist dives into 10 influences that shaped Allura’s costume design, from Kim Kardashian herself to classic Bond girls to a baker-boy cap she had to fight for.

[For Season One,] no one gave us any sort of visual references. I’d never been a costume designer before, but usually, when I’ve worked on character-based stuff—I used to work a lot with Nadia Lee Cohen—we would develop the character first, so when I go into styling, it’s quite easy to visualize that person. There was no time for that, and there was no visual reference for Allura. So, when I came in, I was just thinking, “Okay, what do I see her as?” I looked at Kim personally, and she’s this fantasy. There is no one like her. So I’m like, “Okay, if you have all of the money in the world and all of the access in the world, all the power in the world, what would you look like?”

I’ve always thought of Kim as someone that creates and shocks and is the first to do something, whether people like it or not. And that’s the energy I wanted to go into this with. She challenges the norm. I’ve also always been a big believer in trying to be a trendsetter rather than a follower. In some of these moments, I would find it difficult to make a mood board because the references weren’t there. That was where I would say to Kim, “Trust me.”

For Season One, we started reaching out to brands, and Jean Paul Gaultier was [the first to say], “Let’s do it.” The minute we had them, the whole narrative of the show changed. They went deep into the archives and started sending these looks that hadn’t been worn in 20 years. It was amazing. We were sourcing vintage and archival pieces on the side, but we really focused on Jean Paul Gaultier because everything else compared to that just looked sad.

When the images started circulating from the first season, a critic that I respect wrote that the fashion was “kind of obvious.” Although I disagreed, I understood where he was coming from. Maybe Kim wearing a Mugler suit that accentuated her body and her curves was something we had seen her wear before. This season, I wanted to do something really different. I wanted to go less out-there with the colors and a little less predictable with the shapes and the styling. I picked designers and pieces that weren’t obvious choices, and I think it showed growth from last season. We took it somewhere else completely.

For hair and makeup references, I would go on Pinterest and pull old Helmut Newton shoots because I wanted it to look very timeless. I think that was the key. Everything looked … not minimal, there was definitely no minimal… but very ’90s-based and gorgeous.

Kim loves Madonna. There were a lot of old Madonna images for her platinum hair and makeup, a lot of which was done by Pat McGrath.

I think I’d made a point this year that I wanted to take it down a dark route. I almost wanted Allura to be like, “I just had a shit experience in the first season My man’s left me. I’m pissed off. I’ve gone away, and I’ve come back, and I’m serious now.” So everything’s much more covered up. There are darker layers. She’s experimenting. When I was going through the [recent] runway shows, everything was very pink and quite eclectic, so we mainly went with archival pieces. I used a lot of really rare Yohji Yamamoto, Comme des Garçons, and Lee McQueen.

There was a Margiela skirt suit [from the Spring 1994 collection, Retrospective] and it was just very kitsch. It was a long skirt with a suit jacket and was paired with a black leather baker boy hat from Hermès. We were in a bidding war with a collector in Japan. When we were filming the scene, someone in production or maybe Ryan [Murphy] had mentioned they wanted to tone down the hats for this season. But I got that memo after styling the look, and I was adamant that the look needed the hat. I begged. And then she sat at the table wearing a hat and I could see everyone was like, “How is she getting to wear a hat?” I just felt like the look needed it. It took [Kardashian] out of her normal visual stance and how she usually portrays herself.

Comme des Garçons is very destructive and not really tailored to fit a woman’s body in the way [Kardashian] usually likes. And we got her in [a gray jacket and long lilac-gray column skirt] and worked it around her body. She never once made any comments. She would just look at herself for a second and be like, “Cool.” And then go on set.

I have to let Kris Jenner take credit for [Elizabeth Taylor’s 1969 Scorpio cape, worn by Kardashian in Episode 1], because that’s her cape. She would come to set all the time and once had a conversation with Ryan about the fact that she has this cape. He was like, “That’s it, we need it.” From there, we shipped in a $10 million diamond that was most similar to what Elizabeth Taylor was wearing [in original imagery]. It was flown into L.A. in someone’s pocket, and we had it on set for like five days with Kim’s security. It’s a piece of history. It’s really special. Each day, it would go to Kim and then back in her car home because no one wanted to take responsibility for it.

In general, a lot of my choices in clothing and costume referenced old Bond films where the women were super sexual and curvy and had Brigitte Bardot hair. There was glamour, sex, opulence, money. The outfits were completely inappropriate and not really wearable in everyday life. But if I’m going to watch a show, that’s what I want to see. I want to be like, “What the hell? What is that?” There were comments about Kim’s clothing being inappropriate in the workplace, but that’s what it should be. It’s fun.

#6861. We’ve Tested Hundreds of Makeup Brands—These Help Cover Zits Without Causing Them

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

You can use all of the best acne products in the world, but if you’re dabbing on makeup that clogs your pores every day, your dreams of clear skin might not be a reality. Makeup makes a huge difference for acne, far beyond how well it covers up your breakouts. Some formulas are filled with emollients that make the product creamy and dewy, which is great for dry skin, but can potentially clog your pores if you’re prone to breakouts.

A few years ago, I ran every single one of my makeup products through the pore-clogger checker from Acne Clinic NYC. And to my dismay, many of my favorites just weren’t right for my skin. Since then, I’ve vowed to only ever put non-acnegenic products on my face, which has come with a lot of trial and error to find the best makeup brands for acne.

A lot of brands have one or two products that work well for my skin, but only a few really take the cake for their entire lines. If one of the below brands launches a complexion product, I can (usually) safely assume that it won’t break me out. Many of the below brands also make high-performing formulas for covering up your acne without exacerbating texture or redness. From lightweight foundations to smoothing blushes to high-coverage concealers, the below brands have makeup for acne down pat.

Cofounded by makeup artist Nikki DeRoest, Ciele was founded with two missions: acne-friendly formulas that put sun protection at the forefront. While everything is rid of pore-clogging ingredients, they do contain SPF 50+. None of these are a replacement for your daily sunscreen, but added protection doesn’t hurt. In fact, sun exposure can irritate skin, potentially trigger breakouts, and even prolong or worsen hyperpigmentation. The brand has a full assortment of skincare-infused products, including the serum foundation, a Cosmo editor favorite.

Ciele Tint & PROTECT SPF 50+ Tinted Serum Foundation

Ciele Blush & PROTECT SPF 50+ Liquid Blush

Ciele Bronze & PROTECT SPF 50+ Bronzing Concentrate

Many with acne and eczema-prone skin have sworn off makeup entirely, assuming that even formula’s suited to their skin type will feel too heavy or make flaking and texture look worse. That is precisely why Amy Liu founded Tower 28, a makeup line infused with skincare that won’t make your skin look worse when you wear it. They’re the first brand to get the National Eczema Association’s seal on a makeup product ever with their Swipe Serum Non-Acnegenic Foundation (which Cosmo editors gave rave reviews).

Tower 28 Swipe Serum Non-Acnegenic Foundation

Tower 28 GetSet Blur + Set Talc-Free Pressed Setting Powder

Tower 28 Tower 28 Swipe Serum Hydrating Concealer

Since their launch in 1995, the ethos of Bare Minerals has always been natural and skin-forward. The brand’s original tagline was “makeup so pure, you could sleep in it.” Thirty years later, the brand is still one of our favorites for covering zits. Obviously, the iconic powder foundation is a go-to. The formula builds up for coverage and sets with a skin-like finish. But they’ve also launched quite a few liquid and cream products that are equally as lightweight with a mix of light to medium coverage.

bareMinerals Original Loose Powder Foundation SPF 15

bareMinerals Complexion Rescue Luminous Skin Tint Stick

bareMinerals Complexion Rescue Natural Matte Tinted Moisturizer Mineral SPF 30

Although Benefit became known in later years for their mascaras and Benetint lip stain, the makeup and skincare brand is pretty invested in pores. In 2010, Benefit launched the POREfessional Primer, a velvety, silicone-based smoothing makeup primer to help reduce the look of texture and pores. They’ve expanded into skincare with the same pore-treating philosophy, but their newest makeup launches have cemented the brand as acne-friendly. The POREfessional Foundation, a Cosmo Acne Awards winner in 2025, and POREfessional Concealer both offer full-coverage without aggravating acne.

Benefit Cosmetics POREfessional Pro Balm Face Primer Pore Minimizer

Benefit Cosmetics The POREfessional Foundation

Benefit Cosmetics The POREfessional 24-Hour Blurring & Full-Coverage Natural Finish Concealer

Beyond offering formulas that are suitable for acne, Urban Decay’s complexion assortment keeps oily skin in mind. You might know the brand from your Naked Palette days (never forget), but they’ve really cornered the market on long-wearing, mattifying base products. There’s a mattifying setting spray, primer, and even an innovative powder spray (!).

Urban Decay Urban Decay All Nighter Ghosting Paste Primer

Urban Decay All Nighter Waterproof Makeup Setting Spray - Matte Finish

Urban Decay All Nighter Ghosting Setting Powder Spray

Although all of Rhode’s skincare isn’t exactly the best for acne-prone skin (it’s suited well for dry and sensitive skin types, because the formulas are all very hydrating, but some of them contain common pore-cloggers), the makeup is surprisingly fantastic. Both the cream blush and bronzer are not only made with acne-friendly ingredients, but they also blend out evenly and make textured skin look smooth. We also love the brand’s mist, which is technically a skincare product, but it melts down your makeup for a dewy finish that won’t clog pores.

#6858. This Colorful California Mountain Home Gives Classic Ski House Style a Fresh New Look

Using the surrounding landscape as palette inspiration, designer Alden Fletcher brought 70s retro flair to this family retreat.

When most people think of a classic ski chalet, they picture log-lined walls, dark corners, a raging hearth, plenty of plaid, and maybe even a taxidermied deer or two. But a certain kind of magic strikes when high-altitude architecture hits the right notes, and this Truckee, California, retreat is a masterclass in making the mountains sing.

Built almost 20 years ago, this family holiday home boasted all the 90s mountain classics: an abundance of knotty pine, traditional slate floors, and a minimalist beige palette. Undoubtedly stylish for the time, the retreat now felt out of step with the Bay Area family looking to make it into a personalized winter escape. The owners knew updates were imminent (necessary, even), but they feared losing the home’s classic California soul. Fighting modernism’s white-box aesthetic, they called in Alden Fletcher of the eponymous San Francisco-based design firm to meld the home’s retro architecture with a fresh, forest-inspired brief.

Noting the owner’s concerns and dedication to sustainability, the project’s goal was not to remodel for grandeur, but to restore the home’s charm. Fletcher’s task was to design around storied memories, the family’s obvious love for winter sports, and their passion for entertaining—all while combining the crisp aesthetic of the alpine wilderness with cozy textiles and natural details to create the ultimate mountain sanctuary. By pulling saturated hues from the surrounding landscape and sourcing perky patterns inspired by the local culture, the redesign breathes new life into the slope-side abode without sacrificing sentiment.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Alden Fletcher Interiors Stylist: Beth Protass Location: Truckee, CAThe Space: A five-bedroom, four-bathroom home across 3,000 square feet

The home's hallways set the tone for the entire design scheme. On the first floor, just beyond the main door, wall-mounted ski runners double as decor. Upstairs, Fletcher redefined the boundaries of the primary suite to carve out a nook for a vignette displaying the home’s most bespoke decor piece: a vintage cherry-red chairlift. “The home’s location is unique,” adds Fletcher. “It sits on a ski slope, and you can view chairlifts out the back windows!”

A true gathering space, the living room was designed around the original stone-faced fireplace, though Fletcher did replace the former pine mantel with white oak, connecting the floor details with the exposed oak beams running along the ceiling.

Swiveling burnt orange armchairs accompany an inky indigo sectional. Both gather around a button-tufted ottoman, which doubles as a coffee perch or game table. Shaded sconces flank the fireplace to give the room a glow, even when the hearth is unlit, while a pair of vintage ski shoes hang on the wall, crowning the design.

In the playroom, the deep green hue of the wallpaper paired with the natural texture of knotty pine borrows inspiration from the forest-like landscape just beyond the window. Vertical lines carried through both the wallpaper and the wainscoting create the illusion of height, while playful ski motifs bring the local sport’s culture into the play space.

“The clients are sustainably minded and didn't want to get rid of what was working,” says Fletcher. With conservation in mind, she opted to keep the original perimeter cabinets, refreshing them with paint, new hardware, and Virginia Black Satin Granite countertops, a sturdier alternative to show-stopping soapstone.

To optimize the space, she nixed the two existing islands, reimagining the room to fit a larger iteration that boasts a sink and easily seats five. The design team then added upper cabinets, transforming the space into a storage haven. Later, she placed a library ladder and golden hardware for easy access and a touch of whimsy. Then, she turned her attention to making sure the lighting was up to par. Fletcher firmly believes a beautiful space should be equally as functional. To ensure no detail fell short, she installed discreet recessed lights alongside under-cabinet lighting, and two large glass pendants to adorn the space. All dimmable for ambiance—of course. “There’s nothing worse than a home kitchen that is just flooded with bright light everywhere. A kitchen should still feel cozy and warm, and good lighting is the key to accomplishing that,” says Fletcher.

Fletcher compensated for this former powder room’s lack of windows with vibrant wallpaper to energize the space and make the most of interior lighting. Borrowing space from an adjacent closet, she converted this former two-piece bath into a full retreat with a shower, vanity, and storage, finishing the space with gold hardware for an additional touch of luxury.

Perhaps the most customized room in the home, the mudroom boasts bold color and smart storage solutions for a space as functional as it is chic. The custom cabinet is an ode to smart design, featuring bench seating, pegs, an overhead shelf, and under-seat shoe storage—but the real showstopper is its bold red hue.

“My favorite element of the built-in is the color. The brick color has just the right amount of earthiness,” says Fletcher.

The family loves to entertain, often hosting large groups of friends and family. To accommodate their social lifestyle, Fletcher designed a hospitality hub. Removing a bathroom door, she altered the room’s layout, allowing space for this guest suite to come to life.

With the new dimensions secured, she covered the walls in printed wallpaper inspired by the great local outdoors. The bold pattern set the tone for the rest of the design as she pulled colors, patterns, and themes to complement it.

For the primary bedroom, the theme was warmth: a warm bed, warm home, warm soul, all channeled through muted terracotta walls. But before it could truly transform, the team had to iron out a few oddball details.

“This room used to have a few odd details, including transom windows to the adjoining bathroom and an exterior door to a deck. We removed those elements, which allowed for a better furniture layout and a cozier feeling,” says Fletcher.

The room features a traditional four-poster bed in a nearly black finish, which carries over to the lighting details and furniture hardware, bringing out the warmth and depth of the surrounding rich red hues.

For the primary bath, the team capitalized on the luxury of the sloped-side views, framing a classic 400-pound slipper tub with a nearly floor-to-ceiling window featuring vintage pine trim. Truly the focal point of the design, Fletcher finished the window treatment with woven shades to tie in the natural wood details of the home and stand up to moisture. Natural grain runs through the room’s doors and vanity, topped with soft white Calacatta marble, channeling the classic ski chalet aesthetic. Gold fixtures extend from the vanity to the lighting, towel rack, and tub, for a touch of glamour.

Boasting the project’s biggest splurge, a custom bunk-trundle bed, the kid’s room combines playful hues with forest-inspired details and maximum sleepover potential. “We wanted to maximize sleeping spots in this home,” Fletcher explains. “The Casa Kids bunk bed in the kid's room was a splurge, but it can sleep 5!”

Alden Fletcher first fell in love with design while working as an art teacher and refurbishing furniture in her downtime. In 2020, she grew that passion into a full-service design firm focused on bringing functionality to storied designs and creating ultra-personalized interiors.

#6854. Stanley Tucci Reveals His One Holiday Tablescape Rule That’s Not What You’d Expect

His holiday palette surprisingly restrained.

Hosting on an ordinary night can be stressful, but holiday hosting takes it to another level. Between curating the perfect themes, taking time to honor every tradition, and packing the schedule full of activities, it’s easy to muddle your aesthetic amid all that prescribed cheer.

Luckily, there are easy fixes. Actor, filmmaker, and trendsetter Stanley Tucci recently sat down with House Beautiful to share his top tips for hosting with style. His holiday hot take? One traditional holiday hue should play a supporting role, not take center stage.

“I love autumnal colors, so I do that, obviously, a lot. We'll do that for Thanksgiving, then Christmas will be more white and green, with some red, but not too much,” says Tucci.

An arbitrator of taste on and off-screen, Tucci believes the only place red hues should take on the holiday table is in their natural habitat, perched on a branch.

“Berries on branches. That's all the red you need,” he says.

Tucci describes his hosting style as classic, simple, and timeless. From the menu to the tablecloth, he steers clear of flashy, overdone clichés—which is reason enough for you to ignore the trend cycle this holiday season (looking at you, Ralph Lauren Christmas), and decorate with what you already own and love.

This year marks the fifth anniversary of Tucci’s partnership with S.Pellegrino, which began with an annual limited-edition bottle featuring his engraved signature. The line has since expanded to include “Dinner Dialogues,” with conversation starters on each label. The 2026 collection continues the concept, with designs inspired by Tucci’s minimalist, curated style. It features three bespoke labels—and the actor admits he has a favorite.

“‘What memory makes you smile?’ I love this one,” Tucci says.

With a little help from Tucci, your table’s style—and the conversation around it—will be elevated and effortless.

#6853. The Women of ‘RHOSLC’ Should Let Bronwyn Newport Have a Little PR Relationship, as a Treat

If this is what it takes to recover from a dissolution of a marriage, so be it.

In an era of reality television where the fourth wall has essentially become a pile of rubble, Bravolebrities have a new favorite game: calling each other out for self-producing storylines. And Bronwyn Newport of The Real Housewives of Salt Lake City is the latest to find herself tied to the stake in this authenticity witch hunt.

Her fellow Utahns seem to believe her relationship with 33-year-old model Brandon Good—which began two months after she announced the end of her nine-year marriage to Todd Bradley and fizzled out before the season aired—is a publicity stunt.

As expected, Reddit threads are absolutely flooded with receipts, proof, timelines, and screenshots supporting both sides of the argument. But there’s one response that I’ve been surprised to see missing from the conversation, and that’s a GIF of Arrested Development’s Lucille Bluth enthusiastically boasting “Good for her!”

If you’re not fluent in reaction memes, let me explain.

It doesn’t really matter if this was a bona fide relationship rebound or a carefully-calculated chess move. In either case, this alleged ploy is exactly what we all need from this show. Brandon is a fun addition to the mix. His mere presence ruffles the cast’s feathers in a very low-stakes manner, which is a welcomed reprieve in a season that’s getting darker by the week.

It’s silly to see Bronwyn sweat bullets through feathered couture while her unfiltered mother, Muzzy, grills Brandon through every second of their probably-too-soon first encounter. And when Meredith Marks and Lisa Barlow crooned through their first live performance of Hypocrisy and Bullshit, it was objectively hilarious that sweet little Zoolander found the song to be catchy before realizing it was an attack on his very own girlfriend.

No matter how Bronwyn may have chosen to navigate the months following her divorce, it would have naturally become a topic of conversation on the show. Sharing personal updates with viewers is the whole entire scope of the role for the ladies of The Real Housewives.

If she had decided to play coy and keep her romantic life out of the spotlight, she’d have been accused of hiding something. But because Bronwyn opted to bring a new fling into the mix, she’s faced with having to beat the PR relationship allegations. And we all know that if she went for a secret, more sinister third thing—like getting really into aerial yoga or launching a luxury candle company—her cast members would have given her the same skepticism.

At the risk of getting too inside baseball, couldn’t one argue that a Housewife accusing her fellow cast member of faking a relationship for a storyline might also just be attempting to pull a narrative out of thin air? And furthermore, isn’t that what we’re all expecting these women to do when that sprinter van drops them off at a vacation rental in Palm Springs?

For 20 years, The Real Housewives have been giving us storylines that are barely tethered to reality. We’ve heard talk of clothing lines that never came to fruition, witnessed vow renewals that were just a divorce detour, and watched waiters hover around dinner tables where the goal is to unpack drama rather than to eat.

Whether Bronwnyn was acting in good faith or scheming, she read the brief, rolled up her designer sleeves, and came to work to get the job done. If you’ve ever fired off a “no worries!” Slack when you were, in fact, worrying, I think you can give her a pass on this one.

And let’s not forget that Bronwnyn spent last season dodging rumors that her 60-year-old estranged husband had farted while “making out” with another woman. If rolling up to the function with a boy toy who may or may not be sending an invoice her way afterwards is what it takes to recover from such a stinky dissolution of a marriage, so be it.

#6852. Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce Re-Created One of Their First Public Moments After the Chiefs’ Win

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce made a rare move following the Kansas City Chiefs’ win over the New York Jets yesterday. The newlyweds left Arrowhead Stadium together, re-creating one of their first public moments as a couple by driving off in a vintage Cadillac Coupe DeVille. Onlookers filmed the happy couple waving to the crowd as they departed.

When Swift attended her first-ever Chiefs game in September 2023, the pair did the same thing, leaving in Kelce’s 1970 Chevrolet Chevelle 408 Stroker convertible. They seem to have a thing for the classic cars, and even raffled off a 1970 Chevelle at their wedding, an apparent homage to the 2023 moment.

The singer attended Kelce’s game against the Jets with her parents and her friend Karlie Kloss. It marked Swift’s first appearance in years with Kloss, who also attended the singer’s wedding in July.

Swift usually leaves Arrowhead Stadium undetected. Back in October 2025, she told BBC Radio 2’s Greg James that her greatest skill as a spy would be “entering and exiting the building without being seen. Just put me in a garbage can, roll me, I don’t care. Honestly, I can fit in like, a purse. Sometimes I just can’t deal with it [the paparazzi and attention] and in those times, I won’t deal with it. I’m just digging tunnels under every building I go into, airlifting in through the skylight.”

In January 2025, Parade spoke with Ty Richmond, president of event services at Allied Universal, who detailed how intricate plans can be to get VIPs in and out of football games. “When you have a very high-profile individual [coming to an event], the logistics of that person getting into the venue and getting into the specific location where they need to sit is a job and a process in itself,” he said, adding that for someone at Swift’s level of fame, thousands of security professionals could be involved in days of preparation.

“If, for instance, there are fans that are known to be targeting [a star] and/or have been a threat in the past because they’re fixated on a talent or a personality, those are all very specialized, very confidential issues that have to be understood and mitigated properly,” he added.

#6851. The 15 Best Beauty Minis That Fit Right in the Palm of Your Hand

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fun-size favorites from Prada, Rare Beauty, and more make perfect holiday stocking stuffers

The ideas that things can be “small but mighty” and that “good things come in small packages” are especially true for your favorite beauty products. There’s plenty of room, metaphorically and literally, to curate a collection of the best beauty minis, perfect for packing in your carry-on or gifting to friends and family for the holidays.

Itty-bitty, often limited-edition versions of rotation staples you know and love are simply adorable, like Rare Beauty by Selena Gomez Soft Pinch Liquid Blush and Prada’s tiniest set of stylish metal claw clips. Plus, these staples are not only aesthetically pleasing, but also won’t take up too much real estate on your counter or in a festive stocking. If you’re looking to expand your collection of hair, makeup, skincare, and fragrance goodies while keeping it small, I invite you to peruse our short list of the best, too-cute-for-words beauty minis.

Sure, your best friend could use regular bar soap, but why not gift them with this distinguished set of four minis instead? This second edition of Soft Services’s Buffing Bar Miniature Collection might be even better than the first and is entirely dedicated to gourmand scents. From the vanilla-coconut cream, espresso-, and citrus-scented Affogato L'Orange to their best-selling and extremely fresh Green Banana bar to try, and more, chances are your friend will be taking a bunch of calls from the bathtub.

A dab on each cheek will do you when it comes to Rare Beauty’s viral, highly pigmented blush, and this set also contains their Liquid Luminizer and Luminizing Lip Gloss in limited-edition shades your younger sister can enjoy. Along with Sofie’s Kylie Jenner-approved Clean Clean Cleanser, your giftee will also discover travel-ready versions of the Mandelic Clearing Serum and Skin Jelly Oil-Free Gel Moisturizer to complete her routine.

What’s more elegant than a Louis Vuitton perfume? A tiny trio of individual Louis Vuitton perfumes. She can rotate between Louis Vuitton’s fresh, floral Tea Storm; woody, warm Ink Mark; and floral, fruit-forward Moon Tale, depending on her mood.

Westman Atelier’s Petite Party set is perfect to toss in your bag for quick touch-ups all season long. Along with mini versions of its highlight and Baby Cheeks blush sticks, the collection also includes mini eyeliner and mascara to cover your makeup essentials.

Any Lip Butter Balm superfan will be thrilled to unbox this four-piece mini set of some of Summer Fridays’ best sellers. This limited edition includes Birthday Cake, Strawberry Soft Serve, Pink Guava, and Cherry flavors your friend can enjoy for weeks, plus supremely hydrated lips.

Here’s a set of branded claw clips that are almost too cute to wear. But if you do, I’d suggest stacking them for maximum hairstyling impact.

The person in your family with an ever-evolving hair and body mist collection can add four more to their list of favorites. Ouai’s mini bundle of beloved scents, including St. Barts, Ibiza, Santorini, and Melrose, can help mentally transport them to their next beachside vacation with just a few spritzes.

Consider this mini Eadem set an added boost to your go-to skincare routine. Inside, you’ll find the brand’s brightening, smoothing Cashmere Peel, plus its Ultra-Calming Mist and plant ceramide and peptide moisturizer. And to top it all off, the collection also includes a tiny tube of Eadem’s cult-favorite Le Chouchou Lip Softening Balm.

Consider this group of minis as part gift, part skincare treat, particularly for vanilla lovers. Your aunt can rotate through three of Josie Maran’s delectable whipped body butters featuring a blend of shea butter, argan, and avocado oils to keep their skin plump and hydrated from the neck down.

She doesn’t need a bunch of clunky equipment to get the perfect cat-eye manicure at home. Le Mini Macaron’s petite manicure kit includes just about everything an aspiring nail tech needs for a fresh look, including an LED lamp, cat-eye gel polish, a magnet, a nail file, and more.

This mini set of YSL Beauty’s Lash Clash mascaras includes black and brown shades for different makeup looks. No matter which shade you choose, each mascara is ophthalmologist-tested for contact lens wearers and those with sensitive eyes.
