#6849. 9 Curb Appeal Trends That Will Take Over in 2027, According to Designers

These are the exterior elements that are getting a major refresh.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Curb appeal is more than having a pretty house.

“It's not just about how a home looks, but how it lives,” says Danielle Faustini, landscape designer and owner of Helix Green Designs. Homeowners and designers alike are stepping up their game when it comes to creating an appealing facade—in some cases, the shift is quite literal, as stairs are becoming an “it” feature.

The latest U.S. Houzz Outdoor Trends study is seeing a shift toward natural elements like wood cladding, beige exteriors, and native plants.

"Professionals on Houzz have shared that homeowners are looking for exteriors that feel grounded and connected to their surroundings," says Annie Thornton, Houzz outdoor trends expert. “Warm beiges and wood siding blend naturally with stone, native plantings and other organic landscape materials, and they suit a wide range of architectural styles. Our studies show that people are moving away from the stark white and cool gray palettes of recent years toward something that feels more timeless, a look that adds a sense of history without tipping into nostalgia.”

Simple upgrades, like a fresh coat of paint on the front door, are also pivoting into colorways anchored in the past. Designer Alexis Warren is seeing a shift toward darker, moodier front doors in shades of green, deep purple, and near-black, like Farrow & Ball’s Paean Black.

Here are 10 curb-appeal-boosting design trends for the year ahead to make your house the envy of the neighborhood.

Introducing a sculptural element—like an outdoor artwork or creatively placed flora—sends a message that your house has a point of view.

“It elevates the sense of arrival and separates your home from other homes in the vicinity,” says designer Lisa Erdmann. To ensure your home truly stands out, look for one-of-a-kind pieces.

For the home pictured above, Robert J. Bell, founder of landscape architecture firm Bell Design, commissioned artisans to create a copper light inspired by a feather. “More than the end of minimalism, the new focus on sculptural shapes brings back a sense of craft, and the expressive and sensory nature that a simple black metal and glass fixture cannot possess,” he says.

To contrast against the patina, Bell chose purple bougainvillea and a sunny yellow paint on the door.

It’s all about adding warmth and texture, and that’s one of the reasons that beige exterior colors have more than doubled in popularity in the last two years, jumping from 10 to 24 percent, according to Houzz.

Think of a palette that extends into the landscape—warm neutrals such as tan, soft cream, khaki, sand, and greige, says Thornton, who also notes that natural wood and wood-look cladding, sandy stucco, and stone are a welcome change.

Adding paneling can mimic the warmth of wood without worrying about upkeep, points out Francisca Alonso, cofounder and CEO of AV Architects + Builders. This trick also helps soften the front elevation. “We like to combine minimalistic modern details with warm tones to make it more inviting and less commercial-looking,” Alonso adds.

A picket fence may be just what your house needs, but that doesn’t mean you have to resort to the predictable white paint.

“Adding a low-profile picket fence is one of the best ways to gently delineate a property line without in any way compromising sightlines of the home,” says Joelle Nesen, principal designer at Maison Inc. “The picket fence has the kind of classical lines that work well with an historic home like this Craftsman in Portland. By not defaulting to the expected white and leaving the fence a natural wood, the picket fence, with its carefully spaced slats that are almost see-through, allows it to blend in with the landscaping and trees that surround the home.”

When you choose gravel over grass or pavers, it’s all about an understated but classy look.

“Gravel and other permeable aggregates like crushed shells are some of my favorite materials for creating more informal outdoor living spaces,” says Faustini. “It is a beautifully tactile and sensory material. Who doesn't love the soft crunch underfoot? And it really softens a space, helping blur the line between outdoor living areas and the surrounding landscape.”

The look works especially well with decking and patinated light fixtures.

Adding native plants around your house feels cohesive, relaxed, and rooted in place, Thornton says.

Rachael Grochowski, founder and principal of RHG Architecture + Design, adds that she expects to see an increase in native landscapes, meadow-type gardens, as well as an integration of clover into lawns.

“The trend to embrace natural landscapes is not only about supporting environment regeneration, but it also supports our wellbeing by instigating wonder, beauty, and randomness,” she says.

Curb appeal is all about first impressions, and homeowners are not taking this feature lightly. Houzz reports that searches for “front walkway” have more than quadrupled (up 357 percent) in the first three quarters of 2026, compared with the same period last year.

Step design, in particular, is one feature that is growing in popularity.

When Sarah Crawford, associate designer with Courtney B. Smith Design, renovated her home, she added steps to her 1920s bungalow to literally and figuratively elevate the experience. The home initially had just one step from the sidewalk to the front door. By adding a series of stairs to gently guide the way to the door, she added a quiet sense of elegance in keeping with the wood-shingled exterior. Key to the transformation was selecting materials that reflect the character and style of the original home, in this case reclaimed granite. Scale and balance were essential to adding a series of stairs to gently guide visitors to the door.

“Steps should never be narrower than the doorway,” Crawford says. “In fact, the wider the steps, the more inviting the approach.”

For a different approach to the front entrance, consider stone pathways, especially those with a casual look, like grass pavers.

Interior designer Darci Hether likes large-format bluestone pavers, which offer a clean, timeless look. “The generous scale of the stones creates a sense of openness, while the greenery between them softens the overall appearance and adds a beautiful, natural element,” she explains.

Erdmann encourages her clients to forgo safe neutrals and treat the front entry door as a place for personality.

“Whether it is hot pink, lipstick red, cobalt blue, or Benjamin Moore’s color of the year, Webster Green—make a statement!” she says. “Don’t match the door to the shutters and choose something fun. A front door is the easiest, lowest-risk place to experiment since it takes one afternoon and a quart of paint.”

Case in point, Elizabeth Cross-Beard, interior designer behind Palm Henri, took to a vibrant peacock blue to accentuate the lines of the above 1925 French manor house. “When paired with natural materials and muted tones, [the paint] brings just enough color to intrigue the eye without disrupting the overall sense of classic grandeur. Unlike other blue tones which can call too much attention to them, this rich color is the front-door sweet spot,” Cross-Beard explains.

Go beyond ho-hum pots that stand sentry at your door. Look for large pieces with personality to anchor the entrance.

“Aged terracotta, weathered stone, or antique urns bring patina and character, helping even a newly built home feel established,” says MK Smith Boykin of Smith Interiors. “With that kind of presence, the planting itself can be quite simple.” The details are especially important when your home has architectural symmetry, like columns.

“Using matching plants only increases the allure of perfect visual balance,” Nesen adds. “The key is that the scale of the planters needs to match the proportions of the door and porch: Selecting pots or containers that are too small will immediately drain the drama and beauty of the front door statement.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

It’s a longstanding partnership for good reason.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The idea of a uniform isn’t new. In fact, that’s kind of the point: it prioritizes a sense of timelessness that defies trends. The winning partnership that emerged from recent street style sightings, however, felt newly relevant—not a flash in the pan coupling, but one that has what you might call an unshakable bond: jeans and blazers.

The beauty of this particular uniform is that it doesn’t come prepackaged. Instead, you can pick from a plethora of different jeans—are you a barrel-leg, capri, or boot-cut kind of person?—and then opt for the blazer that best suits its proportions.

Keep scrolling for the seven combinations that have been cherry-picked for the insouciant energy that’s part and parcel of the jeans-and-blazer look.

Military jackets were spotted across the street style scene during SS27 shows. Combining the statement piece with casual denim works to balance out the look that will work well into winter.

Here, a pair of ’70s-inspired, front-pocket jeans are the perfect match for a double-breasted, cropped blazer. Plus, choosing a straight-leg silhouette helps to accentuate the padded shoulders of the jacket, allowing a simple look to transform into a statement.

A smarter pair of baggy jeans—either because of a darker wash or lack of distressing at the knees—is the perfect partner for a suede jacket, which requires no introduction after its dominance on the pavements of fashion month.

For a super-polished take on the combination that’s suitable for work (and whatever comes after), a scarf blazer paired with straight-leg jeans is the ultimate power couple.

Like capri pants, Bermudas are still a viable option in autumnal temperatures. As Gabriella Karefa-Johnson shows, you’ll want to wear your pair with a nearly knee-high boot, especially when it contrasts with a patchworked top half that limits the amount of bare skin exposed. Smart.

The best way to remove the slouch factor from cuffed jeans? Add a single-breasted pinstripe blazer with one button fastened.

The curvaceous nature of a barrel-leg jean strikes a brilliant contrast with a blazer that’s cropped to either the waist or hipbone, especially if, like this streetstyler, you keep the rest of your look pared-back enough to let its sculptural lines sing.

#6837. When Does Flirting Become 'Microcheating'?

We often think of cheating as this big act of infidelity that involves sex with someone that isn’t your partner. But sometimes, even things that don’t feel like that big of a deal—liking someone’s Instagram posts consistently, sending a flirty text and then deleting the message off your phone, getting a phone number while you’re out—can break trust between a couple. This is because betrayal and infidelity can only really be defined by two people in a relationship. And sometimes, your partner would call what you’re doing secretly “microcheating.”

Therapist Debra Feinberg, LCSW explains that microcheating consists of “small, often secretive behaviors that cross an emotional line without being a full affair.” Depending on the couple, this can look like anything.

“There is no universal definition and no checklist of behaviors that automatically tell you someone is cheating and what to do next,” clinical psychologist Dr. Paulette Didia tells Cosmopolitan.

Microcheating can lead to difficult discussions and sometimes even a breakup. It essentially becomes up to you and your partner to figure out what boundaries exist in the relationship, what loyalty looks like to you, and how you move forward (or don’t) when trust is broken—no matter what the scale.

Flirting is fun! Exchanging a hot glance with a stranger or acting coy with a coworker—fun, fun, fun. But there’s a point where—if you’re in a relationship—flirting can cross the line into a form of microcheating. And oftentimes, you’re very aware of where that line is.

“The line isn’t the act, it’s secrecy and impact,” therapist and matchmaker Kristen Anthis says. “A useful test is whether you’d do it if your partner were watching, and whether it’s moving emotional energy out of the relationship.”

You may not think what you’re doing is a big deal, but if you know that it’s something your partner would feel betrayed by, that’s a pretty good indicator that they might consider it microcheating. Regardless, the best thing to do is to have a conversation about what you and your partner expect of each other when it comes to interacting with people outside of your relationship.

Licensed Marriage and Family Therapist Cheryl Groskopf explains that couples often face roadblocks around discussions of infidelity because they just assume that “loyalty” means the same thing to them as it does to their partner. The reality is that we’re all very different and think differently about what cheating is.

“Maybe you grew up watching your dad flirt with every waitress while your mom laughed it off, so to you flirting is just being friendly,” she says. “But your partner grew up watching a parent have an affair, so those same interactions feel threatening.”

This is why talking about what “respect” looks like in the relationship is important, even if you swear it’s obvious—because the reality is that it’s not.

Anthis suggests making the boundaries you draw specific and mutual—not guesses. What does microcheating look like to each of you? Now, what your partner thinks is microcheating will likely look a little (or a lot) different from your definition. You might personally think liking certain photos of other people is harmless. But once you hear your partner out and why they feel disrespected, what do you gain by continuing the action? If it really won’t affect your life and it will make your partner feel secure, there is a simple solution there.

But sometimes, the request may not be reasonable at all. Your partner may think texting a friend or interacting with someone outside of the relationship is totally unacceptable. It’s fair to disagree with this. A situation like this is when you should aim to understand whether you and your partner can see eye to eye or not.

“I think people get so focused on what their partner should or shouldn't be allowed to do that they forget to ask themselves what they actually need in a relationship,” Groskopf says. Maybe you're perfectly comfortable with certain interactions but draw the line somewhere else. The key is to just say it straightforward and address what feelings come up for both of you.

“Your partner still gets to make their own choices, and you get to decide whether those choices work for the kind of relationship you want.”

Sometimes you’ll find you can move forward—and other times you’ll learn that you and your partner aren’t really compatible.

The “micro” in microcheating doesn’t necessarily mean that you can just easily move on once a betrayal has taken place. Like with anything important in a relationship, it’s going to take a lot of communication.

If you feel like your partner’s actions reflect microcheating, Groskopf suggests understanding the behaviors before jumping into accusations. “Are they enjoying being desired? Are they sharing things with this person that they're having trouble sharing with you?” she asks.

“I think it's just as important to talk about what that behavior brought up for the hurt partner because sometimes it's activating something much deeper around trust, rejection, or feeling replaceable.”

When the person microcheating approaches the conversation with curiosity about their own behavior instead of jumping to just defend themselves, repair is definitely possible. As long as they understand how their behavior affected their partner and how to change it, the relationship can move forward with trust and respect.

“I'd pay very close attention to how someone responds when you tell them they've hurt you,” Groskopf says. A healthy relationship is one where both people take responsibility for their actions—and especially how they affect the other person. But if there is a pattern of disregard and disrespect, that might be an indicator that the microcheating is just the beginning of your problems.

Don’t let the name fool you: Nothing about “micro” cheating is small or irrelevant. Even the most seemingly harmless acts can cause huge rifts in a relationship. And at the end of the day “cheating” is still part of an equation—and no relationship where someone is betraying the other is in a good place. So you can either discuss (and uphold) boundaries, or watch your microcheating become a major problem.

#6832. Alfonso Ribeiro and Julianne Hough’s Exact ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Salaries Revealed by Show Source

Ever wonder how much Alfonso Ribeiro and Julianne Hough make for hosting Dancing With the Stars every season? Well, some interesting information just dropped, and by that I mean we now know their exact (alleged!) salaries, including a pretty big pay discrepancy.

An insider tells The Sun that Alfonso makes $1.75 million per season for hosting the show. Meanwhile, Julianne makes $1.25 million. If you’re wondering why Alfonso is earning $500,000 more than Julianne, apparently it’s because he’s been hosting for longer, is the “head host,” and has presumably had more time for salary negotiations and raises.

“Alfonso has also been hosting since 2022 and brings his own history with the show, having won Season 19,” the source says. “His experience in Hollywood, his admiration from viewers and his established relationship with the franchise support the substantial hosting fee.”

The source adds that Julianne “brings a different kind of value to the show than Alfonso. Her value comes from her being a two-time DWTS champion and a former judge. She also has a lot of Hollywood credits under her belt. But Alfonso is considered the ‘main host’ so he gets more than Julianne.”

Meanwhile, Alfonso and Julianne’s predecessor Tom Bergeron was reportedly earning $150,000 per episode according to Celebrity Net Worth, which is about $1.8 million per season.

Alfonso and Julianne haven’t confirmed their salary details (obviously) and Dancing With the Stars keeps these things under wraps, so take all this with a grain of salt but...they’re clearly earning a lot!

#6829. All the Celebrity Looks From the 2026 LACMA Art+Film Gala

From Jordyn Woods-Towns to Anya Taylor-Joy to Odessa A’zion

Every year, the art, film, fashion, and entertainment industries head to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art (LACMA) for an evening of glamorous fundraising, celebration, and live music. The Art+Film Gala, as it’s called, has seen everyone from Kim Kardashian and Kendall Jenner to Charli XCX and Troye Sivan to Cindy Crawford and Kaia Gerber, welcoming models, actors, philanthropists, musicians, athletes, and more.

Since its inaugural edition back in 2011, the star-studded event has supported the museum’s efforts to expand its programming, making film more central to its exhibitions and education. With sponsorship from Gucci, it has placed a focus on the intersection of art and film, honoring painters, cinematographers, and directors alike for their global contributions. Tonight, it recognized the work of Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic paintings exploring the complexity of nature, and Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. Blackpink’s Rosé was tapped for an intimate performance, and, for the 15th year, Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow were appointed co-chairs.

As for the guests, Chris Burden’s public Urban Light installation—a collection of oversize street lamps that doubled as a red carpet for the evening—saw the likes of Alex Consani, Nicole Kidman, Kylie Jenner, and Paris Hilton. The familiar faces were joined by Anya Taylor-Joy, Alix Earle, Andrew Garfield, Benito Skinner, Candice Swanepoel, Chloe Kim, Emily Ratajkowski, Jordyn Woods-Towns, Julia Garner, Lindsey Vonn, Monica Lewinsky, Odessa A’zion, Rosie Huntington-Whiteley, Salma Hayek, Viola Davis, and more.

Ahead, see all the celebrity looks from the 2026 LACMA Art+Film Gala.

#6827. Cosmopolitan’s 2026 Acne Awards Are Here! 107 Best Products, Treatments, and Tools, Hand-Selected by Editors and Experts

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

The painstakingly picked list features the best pore de-gunkers, devices you can use at home or with a pro, and makeup that masks, to name a few.

Dealing with acne is about so much more than battling the occasional breakout or two. We’re all too familiar with it at Cosmo, so we take treating acne in all its forms seriously. The proof is in our fifth annual Cosmopolitan Acne Awards, which is our biggest yet. Obviously, we’ve given prizes to spot treatments, cleansers, and moisturizers galore, but we go even deeper with everything from makeup that treats and hides zits to an acne-prone scalp savior to devices you’ll want in your bathroom and ones you’ll have to see a pro for.

Choosing 107 winners as the best acne products of the year is no easy feat, so we reached out to top dermatologists and aestheticians for their favorites. The Cosmo team put hundreds of products, tools, and treatments to the test to see what kept our skin clear, smooth, healthy, and happy.

Read on for the complete list of Cosmopolitan’s 2026 Acne Award winners, organized by skincare, makeup, bodycare, and at-home tools and pro treatments. Then keep scrolling to meet our expert panel and learn how we chose all these top picks.

“Taking off makeup is arguably the most important step in any routine to avoid breakouts, and this cleansing balm is my go-to for getting every last bit off, all the way down to my mascara. It melts into a nice, creamy texture and skips the chalky feeling that many cleansing balms have. After adding some water and rubbing, it leaves my skin feeling hydrated, fresh, and ready to start the rest of my skincare routine.” —JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

#6820. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley Goes Completely Backless in a Sculptural Gucci Dress

She made a statement at the 2026 LACMA Gala

At the 2026 LACMA Art+Film Gala, Rosie Huntington-Whiteley was all business in the front, party in the back. Showing up to the Los Angeles County Museum of Art’s annual black-tie event—where everyone from philanthropists to actors to musicians to athletes came together for a glamorous evening of fundraising—the model was seen wearing a sleek black dress with a surprise.

Courtesy of Gucci, the evening’s sponsor, the piece appeared simple and modest at first glance—with a front defined by a sculptural, exaggerated high neckline and a slim-fit long-sleeve, floor-length silhouette. When Huntington-Whiteley turned, however, the dress was revealed to be (almost) entirely open, with a singular piece of silver hardware creating separation between a low scoop back and a high leg slit.

And, if that wasn’t enough, the whole garment was lined with dark-green sequins, which shimmered subtly in the light. She matched the shade to her diamond-and emerald earrings, which were on full display thanks to her slicked-back ponytail. She slipped into pointed-toe black pumps and stopped for photos in front of Chris Burden’s famed public art installation, Urban Light, which doubled as a red carpet for the evening.

The Art+Film Gala celebrated its 15th year tonight, supporting the museum’s ongoing efforts to expand its film programming. It honored the work of painter Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic work exploring the complexity of nature, as well as Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. Blackpink’s Rosé was tapped for a musical performance, and, like every year, Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow served as co-chairs.

#6819. 27 Creative Ways Designers Hide TVs for a Stylish, Screen-Free Environment

These genius tricks keep it accessible yet out of sight.

For years, many designers and design-loving homeowners have felt that having the TV on display is a major faux pas. Even though most of us have one and use it daily, there’s something unappealing about having a big black box against the wall and breaking up the interior flow. It’s become even more of an eyesore now that analog spaces are becoming more popular.

While one solution is to invest in a TV that can disguise itself as artwork, those come at a high price; the other option is to learn how to hide the TV in a way that doesn’t feel obvious or tacky, and thankfully, designers have a lot of ideas.

We’ve also included plenty of inspiring photos and expert tips to guide you along the way. If your goal is to reduce the visual presence of technology in your home, you’re in the right place. Keep reading to find inventive ways to make any room feel screen-free—at least when the TV isn’t in use.

Of course, you can hide your television the same way you'd hide anything else you wouldn't want on full display at all times: tuck it into a cabinet. At least, that's what designer Kylie Buhagiar at Melbourne, Australia-based Golden did. "In the main living room, the TV is discreetly integrated into a wall-hung metal joinery box, with hinged doors that elegantly stack back to reveal the screen when needed, maintaining a clean and uncluttered aesthetic," Buhagiar says. "This thoughtful design transforms the joinery into a sculptural piece of furniture, allowing the space to effortlessly transition from a main living room to a calm sitting area, enhancing the home's rich yet comfortable atmosphere."

The whole point of concealing the TV is to hide it, but sometimes, the mechanism used to hide the screen just draws more attention to the area. Gillian Segal, an interior designer based in Vancouver, British Columbia, came up with a clever solution that basically looks like the wall. "This room functioned as both a formal living space and informal family room (complete with movie nights and lots of kids' TV shows), so we wanted a design that felt both elegant and approachable," Segal explains. "We concealed the TV behind a very simple millwork door system that folds open and closed. It was a cost-effective solution that allowed other elements in the room to shine."

It’s no secret that many designers find the Samsung Frame TV to be beautiful, but designer Ariel Okin takes it a step further by adding a literal frame to the Frame. This hidden-TV tactic ensures that it matches other elements in the space, such as the fireplace screen, thus hiding it in plain sight.

If you don’t want to think about your TV when it’s off, you can cleverly camouflage it. In this living room, designer Emily Del Bello painted a shadowy accent wall black and mounted the TV there—you wouldn't even notice it at first glance.

In a room as traditional and cohesive as this, you probably don't want a big black rectangle interrupting your aesthetic, so do what designer Lynne Uhalt did and hide your TV in a closet. This guest room is heavily inspired by European interiors and architecture, but Uhalt still wanted the guests to be able to watch TV and relax in bed. As a design-friendly solution, she put the TV in one of the closets, which still boasts enough room to enough to hold clothes.

Designer John Wooden maximized limited square footage in his clients' cottage by cleverly hiding several items, including placing their TV in a beautiful credenza. One of the simplest methods to hide your TV is by incorporating it into furniture that already complements the space. This wooden mid-century piece fits perfectly into the room, making it an ideal spot for a hidden TV.

In a maximalist, highly colorful space like this, designer Shazalynn Cavin-Winfrey couldn't see how a TV could work in the room, so she decided to hide it in the green cabinet in the back. However, the designer took it a step further and covered the glass with fabric that pulls hues from the rest of the room.

Everick Brown designed a statement wall encased in custom steel with walnut insets to create a “floating credenza” for a hidden TV. A console table behind the sofa helps separate the two living room zones.

Designed to look like abstract artwork, Kristin Fine hid the TV behind modern paneling above the fireplace. It suits the neutral tones nicely and maintains the refined and grown-up atmosphere while also ensuring that it's family-friendly.

This living room sitting area revolves around the corner fireplace. To ensure that the TV didn't take up too much valuable visual space, designer Denise McGaha invested in a Samsung Frame and disguised it as a photograph that complements the blue and grey color scheme.

This outdoor living room, designed by Amanda Lindroth, is the perfect place to entertain and unwind. Look closely at the pagoda-shaped cabinet above the fireplace, and you'll notice that it opens to reveal a hidden TV. It was based on a design from St. Michael's Mount castle in England.

What do you do in a busy kitchen where the children love TV and the mom doesn't—but she does want to keep an eye on said kids? Enter the Samsung Frame, once again making it all work in family homes with competing interests and preferences, like in April Tomlin Interiors designer Sarah Rogers's kitchen here.

A bi-fold panel painting by Stuart Coleman Budd hides a TV, but the goal was to be transparent. “Bronze hinges honestly express that it’s a movable screen—that this is true kinetic art,” says architect Ken Pursley of Pursley Dixon Architecture.

This formal living room, designed by Mark Cunningham, doubles as a more relaxed and casual hangout room. To ensure that it could do both well, Cunningham built a hidden TV into the back wall shelf that pops up when the occupants want to unwind with a good movie.

Don’t hide it, just make it one with your wall. This gallery wall of various-sized framed prints blends in with the Samsung Frame TV, which has a wood frame and a rotating display of art images. “None of my clients' guests realize it's a TV when they first walk into the room,” says Natalie Myer of Veneer Designs.

Custom paneling over a fireplace elevates the television area in this bedroom lounge zone by Studio McGee. Flush with the fireplace facade and further disguised by a custom mirror, the hidden TV definitely will not be ruining the chic decor scheme here.

Related Story: Shea McGee Just Dropped a Cottagecore Home Collab With a Major Fashion Brand

Designer CeCe Barfield Thompson hid a TV behind a retractable antique mirror for maximum glamour. “In an oak-paneled Manhattan library, I designed an antique mirror to hide the wall-mounted television,” Thompson says. “The mirror's lower panels retract like a garage door to reveal an entertainment system behind. This mechanism allowed us to create a room that was incredibly functional, without sacrificing an ounce of beauty!”

Related Story: This Manhattan Apartment Showcases an Abundance of Antiques and Contemporary Art

Here's another example of custom cabinetry with a hidden TV. A sliding door flush with the wood accent wall makes this media room designed by Heidi Caillier extra discreet and pretty. After all, the custom upholstery, drapery, and grasscloth wallpaper are too good to get lost in the entertainment system shuffle.

Designer Nina Farmer took the most elegant approach possible—hiding the TV in a custom mirrored cabinet atop the mantel. “The living room needed a concealed TV due to the formality of the architecture,” she says. “Hand-blown mirror was used on the doors so that the enclosure fit with the original 1850s marble mantel. It has concealed hinges and no pull, so when it’s closed, you would never know what’s behind it.”

If you can’t outright conceal your screen, the key is to avoid the “black hole” effect when it’s off. “I always try to blend them into the architecture of the house,” says designer Eche Martinez. “We recently completed a project in Belvedere, CA, where the homeowners were hesitant about installing a TV in their living room. To solve this, we decided that the best way to divert attention from the TV when it was not in use was to have an oversized, freestanding piece of art right next to it. Clients loved it, and above all, the piece looks great in the room."

#6817. Merlot Nails Are the Most Luxurious Way to Wear a Red Manicure This Fall

Cozy up with a glass of wine and start reading all about this expensive-looking trend.

I’ve never been a big fan of a classic red manicure on myself, but when it comes to deeper shades, I’m all in. Burgundy nails, black cherry nails, spiced cherry nails... these are all my speed. So, when a new expensive-looking crimson shade came along this fall, I was automatically on board. Meet merlot red nails, the rich, wine-inspired shade that’s giving my usual fall manicure colors some very chic competition. (I love a good glass of red wine almost as much as I love a good fall manicure, so this trend has been especially intriguing.)

These high-gloss, juicy nails combine red with subtle purple and berry undertones for a rich, full-bodied color (much like a full-bodied bottle of merlot). Ahead, I’ll break down everything you need to know about trending merlot nails, including inspo pics and how to tips from nail artist Vikki Swan.

As the name suggests, merlot nails take inspiration from the deep, reddish-purple color of a glass of merlot wine. These manicures use burgundy and dark cherry red polishes that have subtle berry or plum undertones for a rich, multi-dimensional finish. Unlike a bright cherry red manicure, merlot is deeper and more muted, making it particularly great for fall and winter, and also making it super versatile and easy to wear. “Merlot red is a classic in its own way, just like traditional red nails, but with a deeper, more seasonal feel,” says Swan. “The darker wine tone gives the manicure a fall mood and makes it look richer and more elegant. It’s expressive, but still very versatile and easy to incorporate into different fall looks.”

The easiest way to get merlot nails is to find a polish that resembles your favorite glass of vino tinto. Look for deep wine and red-burgundy shades, recommends Swan, rather than a traditional bright red or nearly-black oxblood.

Prep is key for any manicure, but “this is especially important with dark and red shades,” says Swan. “If the cuticle and nail plate are not prepared properly, the final result will not look as clean and polished.” Dark colors emphasize imperfections on the nail and cuticle line, meaning you want to ensure dead skin is removed from the cuticle area and the nail is totally clean. “The cuticle area needs to be cleaned thoroughly so the color can be applied neatly and evenly without flooding the skin,” says Swan.

Once your nails are prepped, apply two thin coats of your favorite merlot polish and finish with a top coat of your choice. A super glossy one works well, but “I also like a matte finish with small gold accents,” says Swan. She also advocates for a merlot French manicure or micro French if you wanna incorporate a little minimalist nail art. Now, top off that glass and check out these merlot red nail ideas that will make your fingertips look absolutely regal.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6815. Nicole Kidman Celebrates the Art of Ballet in a Voluminous Chanel Tutu

She made her LACMA Gala debut in a messy high ponytail

Every year, the Los Angeles County Museum of Art welcomes the art, film, fashion, and entertainment industries to its annual fundraising event. Since its inception back in 2011, the Art+Film Gala, as it is known, has become a staple fixture on the fall calendar, seeing everyone from models to actors to philanthropists to musicians to athletes return time and time again. But this year, the starry festivities also saw a new face or two—including the one and only Nicole Kidman.

Making her LACMA debut, Kidman showed up to the event wearing a voluminous Chanel dress from Matthieu Blazy’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection. On top, the taupe-and-cream piece featured a long-sleeve corset bodice, with a raw-edge neckline that contrasted the structured boning below. From the hips, layers and layers of semi-sheer tulle cut at different lengths created a high-low tutu skirt that, even when still, gave the illusion of movement.

Before heading inside, the Babygirl actor—with a messy high ponytail and open-toe heels—stopped for photos by Chris Burden’s Urban Light art installation, which doubled as a red carpet for the evening. She was joined by the likes of Alex Consani, Kim Kardashian, and Paris Hilton.

LACMA’s annual gala was founded to support the museum’s efforts to expand its programming, making film more central to its exhibitions and education. It has placed a focus on the intersection of art and film, and takes a moment each year to celebrate individuals for their contributions to the visual and cinematic arts. Tonight, it honored the work of painter Vija Celmins, known for her photo-realistic explorations of nature, as well as Dune filmmaker Denis Villeneuve. Blackpink’s Rosé was tapped for a musical performance, and, for the 15th year, Leonardo DiCaprio and LACMA trustee Eva Chow served as co-chairs.

#6813. Designers Reveal the Fastest Way They’ve Updated a Living Room Under $100

A few small swaps can make your space feel brand new.

A living room is arguably the most popular spot of the house, so why not give it the tender loving care it deserves with a design refresh? Problem is, when it comes to most home projects, it’s easy to convince yourself good things only come to those who wait—or anyone willing to fork over a small fortune.

Fortunately, affordability and efficiency are no longer mutually exclusive. According to the pros, it’s possible to elevate a space swiftly and without spending much money. Want proof? Keep scrolling for the clever (and quick) ways designers have upgraded a living room for $100 or less.

Barbra Fordyce of Ginger Lemon Indigo says plants are “hands-down” one of the best low-cost, high-impact ways to spruce up your space. “Bringing something living into your living room instantly changes the mood, quite literally bringing life, nature and an organic beauty into the room,” explains the designer. While fresh foliage can look good anywhere, she loves how plants can add a “verdant richness” to neutral rooms without throwing off the entire color palette.

That said, houseplants aren’t a one-size-fits-all situation, so choose wisely.

“If you have high ceilings, you want the plant to be proportional to those dimensions,” Fordyce says. “If the plant or tree is too small in a large living room, it will feel dwarfed.”

For the perfect finishing touch, use your leftover budget to buy a beautiful planter. “The container should echo the feeling of your room,” she shares. “A basket, ceramic container or even a vintage wooden crate can all make beautiful ‘pots’ for your plants.”

Though there is such a thing as too many throw pillows, the right ones can make a difference. “A pillow and throw refresh can go a long way in bringing new life to your sofa,” explains Natalie Kaelin, an interior designer in Raleigh, North Carolina.

Of course, there’s no need to buy brand-new lumbar and square cushions on a whim: Simply purchase covers for your existing pillows and swap them out seasonally.

Neutral linen layers can conjure coastal grandmother vibes virtually anywhere, while plush, jewel-toned velvets offer an elegant, autumnal look. Come winter, embrace the holiday spirit with flannel fabric or a plaid repeat.

We might spend a lot of time talking about layered lighting or how to pick the perfect fixture, but we cannot stress it enough: Pay attention to your bulbs.

“Daylight LED bulbs are incredibly white, so they put out light akin to doctors’ offices and hospitals,” says Elizabeth Cross-Beard of Palm Henri in Baltimore. “A warmer temperate bulb gives a home a candle-light, cozy feel.” As a general rule of thumb, she recommends buying lightbulbs with a temperature of 2700K and avoiding anything above 4000K.

Cross-Beard says switching your bulbs can effectively “redesign” your space, and it’s shockingly inexpensive. “Depending on how many lights you have, it can cost as little as $50,” she adds.

If you want to upgrade your space without spending a cent, Blair Burton says to start with your furniture.

“Sometimes simply pulling furniture away from the walls and creating a more comfortable conversation area can completely change how a room feels,” the Austin-based designer explains.

After all, just because an armchair has been in the corner of your living room for years doesn’t mean it needs to go there. By doing a room rearrangement, you might end up using it more when it’s in front of a warm, sun-drenched window. Maybe you’ll even realize you need to pick up an ottoman to create the ultimate cozy space.

Another $0 hack? Burton also encourages her clients to “shop their homes.” “It’s practical, of course, but it's also about honoring the things they've collected and loved over the years,” she explains. “Sometimes finding an unexpected new home for a favorite piece is one of the most satisfying discoveries in a project.”

“Adding artwork, whether it's a gallery wall, a single large-scale piece, or something in between, makes a huge visual and personal impact in a living room,” says Molly Torres Portnof of DATE Interiors in New York.

Retailers like Minted and Society6 offer well-priced prints, but you can also reframe photographs and posters you already own. As long as you pay attention to scale and proportion, the ceiling’s really the limit. “Rules are meant to be broken,” Portnof says. “If it feels right and makes you happy, you're on the right track.”

If your living room is blessed with beautiful built-ins, you can fill them with treasures you already own, or buy a few new pieces for well under $100.

“Take the time to create vignettes,” advises Tennessee designer Katie Kiser. “More often than not, I find my clients have all the pieces for a well-choreographed space, but they're styled improperly.”

For an instant yet affordable upgrade, head over to your local antique store to pick up some art books, jars, or a trinket tray.

#6808. Three Ways to Master the Noir Aesthetic

Dior’s American Psycho pieces bring a dark cinematic edge to modern menswear.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Film noir has given menswear some of its most enduring images: the broad-shouldered suit, the long overcoat, the shirt with its collar open just enough to suggest trouble. The clothes are sharp, the colors run dark, and the men wearing them tend to look like they've got somewhere important to be. Even when they don't.

You don't need a private investigator's wardrobe to borrow from the genre. A well-cut suit will get you most of the way there, while a graphic T-shirt or a statement bag can bring the look into the present. Dior's American Psycho T-shirt and Book Tote offer a particularly good entry point, drawing on the cover art of Bret Easton Ellis's novel for a dose of dark literary and cinematic attitude. Here's how to make the aesthetic work in three different ways.

The suit is the natural starting point. Classic noir leads tend to wear clothes with presence: broad shoulders, defined waists, full-length trousers, and jackets with enough structure to hold their shape. You can take those proportions as inspiration without dressing like you've wandered off a 1940s soundstage.

Start with a charcoal or black suit, a white or pale blue shirt, and black leather shoes. A long wool overcoat adds another layer and gives the silhouette a little drama. Pay attention to fit, particularly through the shoulders and trouser leg. The goal is a clean line from top to bottom, not a suit so tight you can't sit through dinner.

For a more relaxed interpretation, replace the dress shirt with a fine-gauge black knit or wear the jacket with dark, straight-leg trousers. The result retains the polish of classic Hollywood while feeling appropriate for a modern wardrobe. Save the fedora for a particularly committed evening.

Film noir has always had a strong visual identity. A graphic T-shirt lets you reference that world without committing to a full suit. Dior's black American Psycho tee, which features embroidered artwork based on the first-edition cover of Bret Easton Ellis's notorious novel, brings a literary reference to a familiar menswear staple.

The trick is to keep the rest of the outfit simple. Wear the shirt with charcoal trousers, black jeans, or a pair of dark, pleated wool pants. Add a structured blazer if you want to sharpen things up, or wear it on its own with leather boots for a more casual look. Either way, let the graphic take center stage.

There's a good bit of irony in wearing American Psycho on your chest. Patrick Bateman, the novel's image-obsessed protagonist, treats clothes as a measure of status and a way to distinguish himself from everyone around him. The reference has some bite, particularly when paired with understated basics rather than an outfit that tries to do too much. You get a little cinematic attitude without looking like you're headed to a costume party.

A bag can change the character of an outfit, especially when the rest of the clothes are fairly restrained. The Large Book Tote features the same book-cover artwork as the matching tee. It has a practical shape, with room for a 15-inch laptop and everyday essentials, and can be carried by hand or over the shoulder.

The graphic gives the bag a personality of its own, so there's no need to build an entire outfit around it. Carry it with a long charcoal overcoat, a dark crewneck sweater, straight-leg trousers, and polished loafers. The combination nods to noir's shadowy palette while the bag adds a more contemporary point of interest. You don't need to dress like a private eye to make the reference land. A few well-chosen pieces will do the job.

Start with a dark suit, tailored trousers, a long overcoat, and polished leather shoes. For a more casual take, pair a graphic T-shirt with dark denim or pleated trousers. Keep the silhouette sharp and the palette restrained.

Mix classic tailoring with contemporary pieces. A fine-gauge knit can replace a dress shirt, while a graphic tee or statement bag adds personality to an otherwise understated outfit. The goal is to borrow from film noir's visual language without dressing for a period drama.

Black, charcoal, gray, navy, and white form the foundation of the look. High contrast, such as a pale shirt against a dark suit, is especially effective. You can also introduce muted brown or olive for a softer take that still feels true to the aesthetic.

Film noir style tends toward dark palettes, sharp tailoring, and an understated, slightly brooding mood. Old Hollywood style is a broader category that includes everything from formal tuxedos to relaxed studio-era tailoring. The two overlap, but film noir offers a more specific and shadowy approach to dressing.

#6805. 33 Bedroom Plants That Bring a Calming Touch to Your Space

Do you have one of these in your room yet?

Want to improve your sleep naturally? Try adding a few plants to your bedroom. Beyond offering charm and a cozy atmosphere, plants have been linked to mental and physical benefits, including sleep. Research suggests that being around greenery can lift your mood, ease stress, and promote a greater sense of well-being. Bringing a touch of nature into your sleeping space may be a simple way to add those positive qualities to the room.

Plants may also help calm the mind and move the body from an alert state toward a more restful one, potentially supporting better sleep. A past study linked indoor greenery with more positive emotions and fewer negative ones, which may help you fall asleep faster. The findings point to a broader connection between greenery and well-being, including its possible role in a restful bedroom. It’s easy to see why biophilic design—an approach that evokes nature indoors—has become a favorite among designers.

Ahead, find the best plants for your bedroom. We made sure the options would thrive in all types of lights, but especially in low light, so that whether you’re a beginner or an experienced green thumb, you will find the right verdant friend to grow.

The Aspidistra elatior thrives in low to bright shade, making it perfect for bedrooms that don’t get too much direct light. A fun fact: This plant historically was referred to as the “bar room” plant or “beer plant” because it could survive smoky, low-light conditions in Victorian-era taverns and halls. It is non-toxic to humans, cats, and dogs so if you have pets this is a great option.

Native to the rainforests of Brazil, the Maranta leuconeura is now a common indoor houseplant. During the day, the oval leaves lie flat to soak up the light and at night, the leaves fold upward like praying hands, hence the name.

For a trailing plant that thrives in bright, indirect light get a wax plant. It appreciates higher humidity levels so if you live in an older home with fluctuating humidity, this is the verdant plant baby for you.

For beginner and intermediate plant owners, the nerve plant is a great option because they thrive in most indoor conditions, remain compact, and are safe for pets. They will signal to you what they need so you can water and choose the soil for it accordingly.

Pilea peperomioides, or the Chinese money plant, is a cheerful-looking, easy-to-propagate plant that is incredibly low-maintenance. It's a great plant for a sunny bedroom. All plant parents need to do is place it in bright, indirect sunlight and water only when the top inch of soil feels dry.

Aglaonema tolerates very low light levels, so it's ideal if you don't have large windows in your bedroom. Water it when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The snake plant doesn't mind low light—and because it's a succulent, it only needs to be watered every week or two.

Purported to bring good luck, the money tree has a nice, modern vibe—plus, it's not fussy and grows quickly. Give it medium light, water it every week or two, and it'll be happy.

The pothos plant tolerates any light level from low to high. (That means it's super-easy to keep alive!) Just don't water it until the top few inches of soil feel dry to the touch—overwatering is the only real way to kill this plant.

Lavender has long been considered beneficial for relaxation and sleep. Although most research has studied the use of lavender essential oils specifically, having a live lavender plant in your bedroom probably can't hurt. It needs bright light, so make sure it's on a sunny windowsill or use a grow light. Water when the top inch or two feels dry to the touch.

This handsome vining plant is a great choice for new plant parents. Its glossy, heart-shaped leaves are beautiful as they spill over the edges of nightstands or bookshelves–and it's tolerant of low light levels. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

This adorable fern is one of the best to raise indoors. Its thick, waxy leaves retain water better than many other ferns that have finer fronds—and it won't leave a mess by dropping foliage every day. Give it bright indirect light and water when the top inch of soil feels dry.

This fun plant looks like an old-fashioned ric rac trim. It will grow like crazy in bright indirect light—and because it's a succulent, it needs watering only every week to 10 days.

This social media darling is striking and easy to grow—it just needs bright indirect light. Water when the top few inches of soil feel dry.

The peace lily will adapt well to low light levels, but it needs bright indirect light to bloom. Water when the top inch or two feel dry.

This cute palm that looks just like a messy ponytail likes bright indirect light. And the bulbous base stores water, so it needs a drink only every few weeks when the soil is totally dry.

This handsome plant boasts a beautiful upright form, so it makes an eye-catching statement in the bedroom. Give it bright indirect light, and water when the top few inches of soil are dry. As an outdoor landscape plant, it will flower, though that's unusual indoors.

If you're looking for low-maintenance houseplants, this is the way to go! It will tolerate low light and is often grown in water. In water, change it weekly; in soil, water so that it stays slightly moist.

This cute tabletop palm will add loads of style to your bedroom. It prefers bright indirect light, but it will tolerate medium light better than many other types of palms. Water when it's slightly dry to the touch.

#6803. Go Inside Miu Miu’s Handbag Atelier In Tuscany

Twenty years in, the brand’s matelassé leather remains a luxury calling card.

March 4, 2006, was perhaps like any other day in the mid-aughts. Myspace was the top website in the U.S., while Beyoncé’s “Check On It” was No. 1 on the Billboard charts. Cult-classic teen mermaid drama Aquamarine had just premiered the night before. And in Paris, during the fall/winter fashion shows, the date would prove to be the unexpected start of a new era—not just for one brand, but for fashion as a whole.

At the 18th-century Left Bank restaurant Lapérouse, Miu Miu presented its first-ever show in the French capital—now its permanent runway home—signaling a crucial differentiation from its sister brand Prada in Milan. Paper-thin knits, ultra-mini dresses, peplum jackets, chunky platform wedges, and mismatched-lace oxfords walked the intimate runway, on models adorned with white eyelashes and poppy-red lips. On the handbag front, an experimental leather-making technique was introduced, one that would become Miu Miu’s signature and a staple of aughts aesthetics: matelassé.

Originating in early 18th-century Marseille, the quilting technique was traditionally used in bedding or upholstery. In Miu Miu’s collection, it was developed in the form of a leather handbag—the Coffer—a slouchy shoulder tote in chocolate brown with a twisted handle and a bold metal clasp. A counterpoint to the youthfully eccentric clothes, the bag itself was a subtle debut, with only three shown on the runway and in only one colorway. Nonetheless, it was an immediate hit, carried by Anne Hathaway, Lindsay Lohan, and Isla Fisher in its early years. For the relatively young but increasingly iconic brand, a calling card was born.

Twenty years since that collection, Miu Miu has transformed its matelassé technique into a rainbow of colorways and style offerings. The seemingly seamless Wander and boxy Arcadie styles boast prime spots in the brand’s portfolio of leather goods, especially when it comes to the matelassé iterations—just take a look at the brand’s 2026 campaign starring Gigi Hadid.

The handbags come to life in Miu Miu’s leather goods workshop in Tuscany, the architecturally award-winning headquarters called The Garden Factory, built in the Valvigna region near where Miuccia Prada’s husband Patrizio Bertelli grew up, and designed by Guido Canali in the mid-1990s. Completed in 2015, the building itself is a physical manifestation of the Prada Group’s minimalist aesthetic.

The Garden Factory’s nickname derives from the property’s one-to-one ratio of building to greenery, a complete balance of cool steel and native vines, and plenty of glass windows to bridge the two. Inside is row upon row of accessories—66,000 leather goods and 77,000 pairs of shoes—carefully filed from the collections over the years to serve as both record and inspiration. Instead of season or collection, the cabinets are organized by product category, and matelassé has its own section.

Despite its creation 20 years ago—or perhaps in part because of it—the very tactility that makes Miu Miu’s matelassé leather so appealing is hitting a peak in current luxury collections across the board. To a consumer, the in-person experience was always a factor, but in the Instagram age, brands are turning to hyper-visual signifiers of craftsmanship to keep up with the market. Be it ASMR-inducing sequins, fluffy feathers, slick latex, or impossibly buttery leather, touch and feel appeal, even through an iPhone screen, is increasingly important. To see a matelassé handbag is to wonder how it feels, and to feel one is to experience its pillowy, permanent spring, an effect somewhere between plissé and origami. That effect is intentional, yet the product itself is softer in practice. That, too, is intentional.

The quilted technique is achieved by layering elastic Lycra with thin padding and nappa lambskin leather, sourced from Miu Miu’s tannery in France. Strategic patterns are stitched onto the layers and then the Lycra is removed, allowing the leather and padding to fold back into itself without losing the valleys created by the stitches. Thus, the matelassé effect comes to life. The smaller the product—like a card holder—the harder it is to craft. 3-D modeling is never used. Leather products are always assembled by hand, an important standard for the brand, and inspected manually by experts who know every detail of the bag’s design.

How does one become an expert in Miu Miu bags, you may ask (besides simply being a loyal fan)? Prada Group has its own leather goods academy where craftspeople spend six months learning about the brands’ luxury practices and standards. Even after those six months, it takes five to 10 years for a craftsperson to know how to make an entire bag themselves, start to finish.

From anywhere in the building, you have a view of the gardens outside, along with 45-degree-angled ceilings that let light in without creating too much heat. Stations are bustling with artisans, most of them women, in teams of two. They wear white lab coats emblazoned with the Prada Group logo.

The building’s minimalism, it seems, isn’t a lack, but an open space for creation to take place. And like the windows bridging steel to vine, or the archives linking past and present, or even the stitched valleys of a matelassé handbag, Miu Miu has mastered the spaces in between.

#6800. 14 Everyday Home Items You Need to Replace More Often Than You Think

These overlooked swaps can make your home cleaner, safer, and fresher.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Your home might be a showcase of timeless design, with stylish accessories, custom artwork, and high-end furniture. But when the everyday things that keep a household functioning are left unattended to for long enough, the space may be less clean and organized (and safe!) than it looks.

“Even the most gorgeous spaces lose their charm if we forget to update essentials,” says Linda Hayslett, principal designer at LH.Designs. “Keeping your home looking and feeling fresh isn’t just about design—it’s also about the little things.”

Caring for your home means looking beyond the interiors. Giving neglected areas the attention they need can keep germs and grime at bay, and replacing certain items can also protect your health and safety. Consider your carbon monoxide alarm: If an aging unit fails during an emergency, the consequences could be deadly.

To help you keep everything in good shape, we asked experts which 14 household items you should replace as soon as possible. Take a look—you can thank us later!

Smoke and carbon monoxide detectors lose their sensitivity over time, which could delay detection during an emergency. The timeframe for when you should replace these will vary by manufacturer. Look up the model you have online to be sure—even a fairly new alarm should be tested once a month. The batteries should be replaced once a year.

Mold and mildew can build up on shower liners, especially in damp environments. A 2024 study by SafeHome.org showed that shower curtains had the most germs of any other surfaces in the bathroom, including the toilet seat. While Hayslett recommends washing them every three months, the material can eventually become brittle over time.

Dingy towels are the antithesis of luxe. Towels lose their absorbency and softness after years of washing.

“Avoid using too much detergent, which can build up on fibers,” Hayslett says. “Use a little vinegar during the rinse cycle to keep towels soft.” If your bath towels feel scratchy or start to smell musty despite washing, it’s time for a refresh.

To prolong the life of your towels, make sure you let them dry properly between uses to prevent mildew growth, noted Vyanca Soto of Market Studio Interiors. And you should definitely swap them out if you notice discoloration from your skincare products.

You likely spend plenty of time in your kitchen and bathroom each day. Make sure you take a few minutes to examine the grout lines around your bathtub and kitchen countertops.

“Grout breaks down over time, and you can end up with water damage to the drywall,” says Rebekah Zaveloff, cofounder and creative director at Imparfait Design Studio. “If you look and there are spots that have pulled away or deteriorated, it’s time to regrout.”

A sagging mattress is a clear sign it’s time for a new one. Mattresses lose support and can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and even mold, which is certainly not a dreamy way to slumber.

“Flip your mattress every six months [unless it's a one-sided design] to help maintain its structure and extend its lifespan,” Hayslette advised.

A mattress protector can extend its lifespan as does regular vacuuming to remove dust and debris. Soto recently bought a bed vacuum cleaner and says, “It works like a charm.” She also uses it on her dog’s bed to help it stay clean and smell fresher.

The lifespan of a pillow mainly depends on its material, whether that be foam, feathers, or another type of synthetic fill. But once a pillow of any kind loses its shape, it's time to say goodbye. Like mattresses, pillows can harbor dust mites, dead skin cells, and sweat over time. You can prolong a pillow's life with a pillow protector—Soto likes the Serta Power Chill—but you should still swap those out every year or so.

When to replace: two to three years for sheets; five to 10 years for duvets

Bed linen care and personal hygiene dictate the condition of your linens. Similar to pillows, sheets and duvets can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and bacteria, especially if you allow kids or pets into your bed.

“Over time, duvets lose their fluffiness and insulating properties due to regular use and washing,” says Rebecca Bobroff of Rebecca Bobroff Design.

If you’re prone to night sweats or use a lot of skin-care products, you may notice that your sheets become quickly discolored. Along with regular washing, you may want to stick to white sheets you can bleach over colors, which will look dingy even when they're clean.

“At a minimum, wash pillowcases once a week,” says Danielle Hurhula, head of home merchandising for Quince. “I’d recommend bumping washes up to twice a week if you suffer from allergies or asthma, sleep with hair or skincare products, or have sensitive or acne-prone skin.”

HVAC maintenance experts, including Energy Star, recommend checking filters monthly and replacing air filters every three months to help your system function properly and keep dust and allergens out of your home.

If you remove your filters and they look extra dirty, you may want to up the frequency. Doing so will not only improve indoor quality but keep your HVAC system from overworking and wasting energy, which could shave a few years off its life.

Water filters can accumulate bacteria, minerals, and other contaminants. This can affect the taste and quality of your drinking water.

“The lifespan of a filter cartridge is influenced by the level of contaminants and particulates in your water,” says Eric Roy, Ph.D., founder and chief scientist at Hydroviv. “Sink filter cartridges should be replaced approximately every six months and are designed to handle up to 720 gallons of water.”

You should also watch for a decrease in the flow rate, which indicates the filter is saturated.

When to replace: five years (or as recommended by the manufacturer)

You likely know that fridge water filters should be replaced every six months, but water lines can build up gunk or start leaking over time.

“Inspect the line for cracks or discoloration every year,” Soto suggests. “Flush it with vinegar and water every six months to prevent mineral buildup and ensure clean, clear water. My mom taught me this and when you see the color of the water when you flush it—you'll never stop doing it.”

Using sharp knives on wooden and plastic cutting boards can create grooves that embed bacteria, even after a dishwasher cycle. One study showed that plastic surfaces have the longest bacteria survival time, and scraping wooden boards with a knife significantly reduced bacterial presence.

Soto disinfects cutting boards with a vinegar solution and uses bleach for plastic boards. “Sand down wooden boards occasionally to remove deep grooves and re-oil them to maintain the wood,” she says.

Sponges harbor bacteria, especially when you use them to clean up raw food such as eggs and meat. In fact, salmonella can thrive on a sponge for more than seven days. Hayslette’s tip: Microwave damp sponges for one minute to sanitize between uses, but toss them after two weeks max.

Keep sponges as dry as possible, or better yet, replace them with dish brushes as these are less likely to breed bacteria, according to the International Journal of Food Microbiology. We like the Curio dish brush because it stands upright on a magnetic pedestal and you can replace the brush heads.

When to replace: three to five years (or sooner if scratched)

The nonstick coating on your cookware can wear off or become damaged over time. This decreases a pan's efficacy and also makes it a health hazard—the coating may start to flake off into your food.

“To extend their life, always use nonmetal utensils and hand-wash your pans instead of placing them in the dishwasher,” Hayslette says.

Not only do latex anti-slip rug pads lose cushioning over time, but they can also start to disintegrate.

“They get tacky and sticky on the floor, which can damage the floor's surface,” Zaveloff explains. Pull up your rug once in a while and if you see that the rug pads are turning dark yellow, start shopping for new ones.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6797. The Best Dressed Celebrities at the LACMA Art + Film Gala

From Nicole Kidman in Chanel to Anya Taylor-Joy in vintage Pierre Cardin

Last night, a glamorous cross section of the art, film, fashion, and entertainment industries gathered at Los Angeles County Museum of Art for its annual Art + Film gala, which fundraises for the museum’s film initiatives, exhibitions, acquisitions, and educational programs.

This year’s guests decided to embrace a bit of play with their looks. The event is sponsored by Gucci; so fittingly we saw a lot of the brand on the red carpet. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley, who walked in the Spring 2027 show, embraced drama in a slinky, backless black gown with a slit not at the front, but up the back to reveal just a bit of bum cheek. When Demna spoke about this collection this past fashion month, he said he was most proud of the structural feats of his jersey dresses, light as air but engineered to hang just so. Whiteley walked the show alongside additional LACMA guests including Alex Consani, in a dramatically twisted full-skirted gown, Elsa Hosk, who wore a gray wool gown with a thigh-high slit, and Candice Swanepoel in a dress with full-length silver fringe and slits up to her hips.

Anya Taylor-Joy, styled by Law Roach, pulled from the Pierre Cardin archives, selecting a embroidered silvery-white slip with a ruffled organza evening coat. Kylie Jenner wore a look from couturier of the moment, Ashi Studios, that featured a halter neck gown with an allover deer-like animal print and an oversize tassel at the back. And Julia Garner wore a sculptural black taffeta gown from Loewe, which she paired with oversized earrings from Lisa Eisner.

Perhaps the most fitting look of the evening, Nicole Kidman pulled a much-talked-about dress from Chanel’s Spring 2027 collection by Matthieu Blazy. The look features a bodice designed to resemble burlap and a layered tulle skirt with darker panels added to create the appearance of age and wear. It references Degas’ 1881 sculpture “Little Dancer of Fourteen Years,” and is evocative of what this gala celebrates: Kidman, a movie star, in Chanel, one of the most important French fashion houses, in reference to Degas, one of the most renowned impressionist artists.

Ahead, we’ve rounded up our 10 favorite looks of the night.

#6795. ‘Love Is Blind’ Star Rob Feeley Releases Lengthy Statement, Claims Fans Saw “a Small Part of a Much Bigger Experience”

Love Is Blind Season 11 star Rob Feeley is breaking his silence after a truly messy season. Quick recap: Rob got engaged to Michaella Giorgio in the Pods, but things went south during a cast-wide bar hang, where Rob told everyone he didn’t care about their relationship. Obviously, Michaella ended things, and Rob has some things to say now that he’s watched the season.

“Now that the reunion has aired and this chapter comes to a close, I’ve been reflecting on everything this experience has taught me,” Rob wrote on Instagram. “I’m truly grateful for the people I’ve met, the memories made, and the personal growth that came with both the incredible moments and the challenges. Looking back, there are moments I wish I had handled differently. There were times when I felt overwhelmed and let my emotions get the best of me. While those moments don’t reflect the person I strive to be, I recognize that they happened, and I take responsibility for how I reacted and where I could have communicated better.”

Rob went on to “acknowledge the hurt I caused Michaella,” saying “she deserved to feel heard, respected, and understood. I’m genuinely sorry for the times I fell short and for how I reacted in certain situations. My intentions don’t change the fact that my actions affected her, and I take responsibility for that.”

Rob also confirmed that he and Michaella have been in touch since the show filmed two (two!) years ago, and that they have moved on with “mutual respect.” He then reminded fans that they only saw “a small part of a much bigger experience, and not every conversation or situation can be fully understood from what makes it onto the screen.”

The burgeoning reality star ended his musings by teasing some “projects and opportunities” he’s been working on, and noted that “There’s a lot to look forward to in the coming weeks, and I’m excited to share what’s next.”

So...guess we’ll see what happens (next season of Perfect Match, perhaps?).

#6793. SNL’s You Can See Everything Parody with Druski Resurrects a Classic Eddie Murphy Bit

Back in 1984, Eddie Murphy had fun with John Howard Griffin’s 1961 book Black Like Me with a Saturday Night Live sketch titled White Like Me. In it, Murphy caked on makeup to pass as a white man, flipping his world upside down. It’s still pretty funny to watch after all these years, even if putting on makeup to resemble another race for a joke is walking a tightrope. So it’s damn impressive that Saturday Night Live just got away with it again, with Druski.

On the most recent Saturday Night Live, guest star Druski was at the center of one of the night’s best sketches (and maybe the only one worth watching), which was also the inevitable parody of You Can See Everything, titled Dru Can See Everything.

The premise is simple: What if, before Nathan Fielder, Druski first filmed the final days of freedom for Elizabeth Holmes? Cast members Ben Marshall and an incredible Jane Wickline co-star and elevate the sketch from a mildly funny “haha” to “holy shit they’re actually committed here.” Clocking in just four minutes long, the sketch runs through the highlights of You Can See Everything’s first hour: Elizabeth (Wickline) and her partner Billy (Marshall) living at home, cooking eggs, showing off the controversial Theranos machine, and even Billy’s barenaked shower. But the one change is swapping out Fielder for Druski. And it makes a huge difference.

You can see everything (the sketch) below.

While Fielder’s social awkwardness and determination to ensnare a charlatan without falling victim himself is part of the movie’s drama, SNL knows that Druski would just call out Holmes and Evans’s bullshit straight up. No hesitation. “I just want people to see me as an ordinary person. Can you do that, Druski?” asks Wickline’s Elizabeth Holmes.

Says Druski, “Based on what I’ve seen so far, I’mma go ahead and say no. Y’all got some of the weirdest, freakiest vibe I’ve ever seen.” Grabbing the bowl of eggs, he then turns to the camera: “Whatever they said this bitch did, she done did that sh-t.” (Remember the eggs scene is like, ten minutes into the movie.)

Funny as Druski is, the real MVPs are Wickline and Marshall, even if all their jokes are about the size of Elizabeth and Billy’s saucer-pan eyes. The now spoofed-to-death “I’m always real” scene gets its SNL roast too, with Druski asking literally point-blank, “Are you trying to f-ck me?” Then, when Elizabeth shows off Theranos’s supposedly miraculous machine, Druski doesn’t hold back. “Listen white girl, I’mma say this with all love, right? This the most Temu ass piece of sh-t I done ever seen in my life.” Cut to “Billy” and his weird lizard.

(By the way, we know Saturday Night Live’s production design is top-tier, but it’s frankly amazing they were able to build a virtually precise replica of Holmes’s oceanfront rental and recreate the lighting conditions to make the shots look passably real. Remember that they shoot these things in under a week. I demand a behind-the-scenes video of how they pulled this one off.)

The sketch peaks at the end, when Druski emulates Fielder’s method for staging reenactments by putting on White Chicks-style makeup (to better understand Elizabeth, of course). There’s no getting around it: the man looks freaky. Uncanny. He’s like Fiona from Shrek in a J. Crew sweater. Which makes the joke’s reveal a funny jump scare. Post-midterms, it’s honestly awesome that this kind of woke comedy is “back” (if it actually ever left). I’m not smart enough to suss out if whiteface is actually acceptable. I just know Druski and the SNL team killed it this week.

#6788. Winter’s Most Wanted: Tommy Hilfiger Holiday Picks for Every Occasion

From sharp knits to travel-ready extras, these are the pieces worth putting on your list.

The sweater that looked fine in October suddenly feels too thin, the jacket you reach for every day isn’t warm enough, and a weekend away calls for more than whatever you can squeeze into an overnight bag. Then there’s the rest of the season: office parties, cold walks across town, slow mornings at home, and the occasional trip out of town. Getting dressed for all of it takes a few reliable essentials.

Tommy Hilfiger has been building its take on American style since 1985, when the brand launched with a fresh spin on classic menswear. Its signature mix of preppy staples, sporty details, and relaxed ease has made it a year-round proposition, with pieces that move from everyday dressing to more seasonal needs. For winter, that means familiar wardrobe staples with a little extra warmth, practical layers for unpredictable weather, and accessories suited to life on the go. These six picks cover the season from the first cold snap to the last weekend getaway. Here’s what we’re putting on our fashion gift list.

A cable-knit sweater is a cold-weather staple, and Tommy Hilfiger’s take keeps the look classic. Made from combed cotton with a traditional cable pattern and ribbed trim, it’s an easy layer under a wool coat or a natural choice for a casual holiday gathering. Pair it with dark jeans or chinos for a straightforward winter look.

For trips, a jacket that barely takes up any room in your bag is always useful. Tommy Hilfiger’s lightweight puffer folds into its own interior zip pocket, making it easy to stash for a weekend away or keep on hand when the forecast is unpredictable. It has thermal insulation, a regular fit, and side pockets, so it’s a practical layer for travel and everyday errands alike.

A good winter evening often calls for staying in, and this sleep set makes a comfortable night at home feel a little more put together. This one pairs a simple T-shirt with plaid flannel pants, bringing a classic cold-weather pattern into the mix. It’s an easy gift for someone who appreciates a slow weekend morning as much as a cozy night in.

A warm beanie is one of winter’s most useful accessories, and this one gets an upgrade with a cotton-cashmere blend. The classic knit is an easy finishing touch for everything from a morning commute to a weekend walk. It’s also a practical gift that will see plenty of use once the temperature drops.

A card holder and keychain are the kind of everyday essentials that make an especially easy gift. Tommy Hilfiger’s leather set brings the two together in one package, with a polished finish that feels a step up from the usual stocking stuffer. It’s a useful pick for someone who likes to keep their daily carry simple.

A weekend away calls for a bag that can handle a change of clothes and a few extra essentials without looking like you’re heading to the gym. Tommy’s leather duffel brings a more polished feel to the overnight bag, making it a smart companion for a winter getaway or a quick trip home for the holidays.
